# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is the longest sūrah in the Qur'an. It holds 286 verses, and it stands at the head of the Madinan period, when the community had a city, a treasury, neighbours of other faiths, and a state to run. Al-Qurṭubī records that the sūrah came down in Madinah in scattered instalments, and that the scholars counted it the first sūrah revealed there. A few of its verses belong to a much later moment. (2:281 — **“then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged”**) came down at Minā on the Day of Sacrifice during the Farewell Pilgrimage, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the verses on interest were also among the last parts of the Qur'an to arrive.

The sūrah takes its name from a story inside it. Moses told his people that God was commanding them to slaughter a cow — (2:67 — **“Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.”**) — and they answered with so many questions that the command tightened around one particular animal. The whole exchange sits in this sūrah, and the chapter is named after it. A book named after a cow is a book at home in ordinary life: fields, debts, nursing mothers, marriage, and the animals a household lives on.

The early scholars weighed its place in the Book. Khālid ibn Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qur'an, al-Qurṭubī reports, and the name suits a sūrah of this size, carrying this much law and this much warning.

Its virtues are recorded in the collections, and Ibn Kathīr gathers them. Aḥmad, Muslim, At-Tirmidhī and An-Nasāʾī record Abū Hurayrah reporting the Prophet's ﷺ words: do not turn your houses into graves, for the house in which Sūrah al-Baqarah is recited is not entered by Shayṭān. At-Tirmidhī graded the report ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ. An-Nasāʾī records Ibn Masʿūd saying that Shayṭān leaves the house in which al-Baqarah is heard, and al-Ḥākim entered that report in al-Mustadrak and judged its chain authentic. Ad-Dārimī records Ibn Masʿūd's advice that ten verses recited at night keep Shayṭān from entering the house until morning: the first four verses, the Verse of the Seat, the two verses after it, and the last three. Ibn Kathīr also carries a report of Sahl ibn Saʿd, recorded by al-Ṭabarānī, Ibn Ḥibbān and Ibn Mardawayh, that everything has a hump and the hump of the Qur'an is al-Baqarah; whoever recites it in his house at night keeps Shayṭān away for three nights, and in the day for three days.

Two passages inside it stand out further. Ibn Kathīr notes that the authentic reports call the Verse of the Seat the greatest verse in the Book of Allah, and he records from Aḥmad the exchange in which the Prophet ﷺ asked ʿUbayy ibn Kaʿb which verse of Allah's Book was greatest. ʿUbayy named (2:255 — **“Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him, the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining.”**), and the Prophet ﷺ said, may the knowledge delight you, Abū al-Mundhir. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the report about the sūrah's closing lines, that whoever recites them at night has them as a sufficiency for him.

The sūrah opens with three kinds of people walking three different roads. Verses 1 to 5 describe the believers. Verses 6 and 7 describe those who reject the message outright, and verses 8 to 20 describe the hypocrites, who say one thing and mean another. Mujāhid counted them exactly that way — four verses, two verses, thirteen verses — and Ibn Kathīr records his statement. From there the sūrah turns to humanity at large, then to the Children of Israel and the long account of their covenant, and then to the laws that shaped a community: fasting, pilgrimage, marriage, trade, loans, interest, and the rules of war. It closes with the prayer of the believers and the answer to it.

One thread ties this sūrah to the one before it. Al-Fātiḥah ends with a request: (1:6 — **“Guide us along the Straight Path”**). Al-Baqarah opens by putting down a Book and calling it a guide. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the two as question and answer — a community asking to be shown the road, and a Lord answering with the Book that shows it.

---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**THE LETTERS THAT OPEN TWENTY-NINE SŪRAHS**

The sūrah begins with three letters set side by side: ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”***. They belong to a family the scholars call al-muqaṭṭaʿāt, the cut letters. They are pronounced one by one, each with its own name, and nothing joins them into a word. Twenty-nine sūrahs open this way, as Abū Bakr al-Jazāʾirī counts them: this sūrah first, and Sūrah al-Qalam, which opens with Nūn, last. Some openings are a single letter, such as Ṣād and Qāf. Some are two, such as Ṭā-Hā and Yā-Sīn. Some run to three, four or five, such as Alif-Lãm-Mĩm-Ṣād and Kāf-Hā-Yā-ʿAyn-Ṣād.

No explanation of these letters reaches us from the Prophet ﷺ, and al-Jazāʾirī says plainly that nothing on their meaning is established from him. Al-Jalālayn settles the matter in a few words: God knows best what He means by these letters. Al-Saʿdī takes the same road and gives the rule behind it. The safest thing is silence about a meaning we have no revealed basis for, joined to a firm belief that God did not send these letters as play. They carry a wisdom we have not been handed, and the Muslim rests there.

**WHAT THE EARLY SCHOLARS MADE OF THEM**

The early scholars still gathered the possibilities, and al-Ṭabarī sets them down with their chains. Qatādah said the letters are a name of the Qur'an, and Mujāhid and Ibn Jurayj are recorded saying the same. Others called them openings with which God opens His Book. Ibn Kathīr reports that Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd held that their knowledge stays with God, and that some scholars said they are the names of the sūrahs they begin, as al-Saʿdī also reports.

One further view comes from the linguists, and al-Ṭabarī reports it as well. The letters are simply the letters of the alphabet, named one by one, the way a father says that his son recites alif, bā, tā — meaning the whole alphabet even though he stops early. Al-Kashshāf adds the small point that fixes the recitation. These are names, like the names of numbers, so they are said by their names. A governing word in front of them can carry them into a case, and otherwise they stand still.

Two sayings from the first generation appear in nearly every book on the subject. Abū Bakr is reported to have said that every book has a mystery, and that the mystery of the Qur'an lies in the openings of its sūrahs. ʿAlī is reported to have said that every book has a quintessence, and that the quintessence of this Book is the letters of spelling. Al-Baghawī records both. Ibn Kathīr counts the letters without repetition and reaches fourteen: alif, lām, mīm, ṣād, rā, kāf, hā, yā, ʿayn, ṭā, sīn, ḥā, qāf, nūn. And the scholars agree on one thing. God did not reveal them as a game. Anyone who says that part of the Qur'an means nothing has made a heavy mistake, and the verse that answers such talk is (3:7 — **“We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord.”**).

**WHY THEY STAND IN FRONT OF A BOOK**

The purpose the later scholars settled on is the one al-Mukhtaṣar states in a single line. The letters point to the miracle of a Book built from the same letters people already speak with, which is why an opening like this is usually followed by a verse about the Qur'an — and here it is 2:2. Ibn Kathīr traces that view through the books. Al-Rāzī related it to al-Mubarrid, al-Qurṭubī related it to al-Farrāʾ and Quṭrub, and al-Zamakhsharī adopted it.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains why the challenge arrives in pieces of one, two, three, four and five letters, and his reason is simple. Human speech is built from such pieces, so the challenge was repeated in every size the doubters used. Abu Bakr al-Jazāʾirī adds a second benefit. The leaders of Makkah were working to stop the Qur'an from being heard, and this odd opening caught their ears; once they listened, the words reached them. Al-Baghawī adds the plainest benefit of the three. The letters ask one thing from us, and that is to believe them and recite them exactly as they came down, which is how the Companions treated them, as al-Qurṭubī also notes.

Picture a builder who has finished a house that has the whole town talking. On the morning of the argument he tips a bag onto the table: a pile of ordinary clay bricks, the kind anyone can dig from the riverbank. Then he says, you have these too. Build it. That is the shape of this opening. The alphabet is the bricks, and the claim is the building.

---
## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A POINTER TO THE BOOK THAT WAS ASKED FOR**

The sūrah puts a Book on the table and speaks about it from a distance: ***“This is the Book!”*** The Arabic opens with a pointer used for something far off, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notices the oddity at once. The Book is right in front of the listener, so why push it away with the far pointer? Al-Ṭabarī records that most of the exegetes read the two words as carrying one sense, and he defends them with a habit of Arabic speech. Once words have been spoken, they belong to the past, and Arabic will point at something just mentioned the way it points at something absent. A man hears his friend describe an event and answers, that is exactly what you said. The talk about the letters has only just ended, so the Book arrives as what was just named.

Al-Ṭabarī offers a second reading of the same words: that the Book may mean the sūrahs revealed before this one, since they belong to the one Book. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gives the reading that makes the whole chapter click. The pointer reaches past the letters, back to the end of al-Fātiḥah, to (1:6 — **“Guide us along the Straight Path”**). The Muslim asked for the road, and the Book that was asked for is here.

**THE DOUBT THE BOOK LEAVES NO ROOM FOR**

The next words deny doubt about the Book: ***“There is no doubt about it”***. The earliest generations were of one voice on their sense. Ibn Kathīr gathers the names: Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd, Abū al-Dardāʾ, Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, ʿAṭāʾ, Qatādah and others, all reading it as a statement that the Book is from God without doubt. Ibn Abī Ḥātim adds that he knows of no disagreement on it. Al-Mukhtaṣar sharpens the point: there is nothing of doubt in it, neither in where it came from nor in what it says.

Al-Saʿdī draws a rule out of the wording that is worth keeping. When the Qur'an denies something in order to praise, the denial carries its opposite along with it. A bare absence is not praise. A house free of sickness is not praised unless it is full of health. So the denial of doubt here carries certainty, and the Book holds the clear knowledge that removes doubt. Certainty is not a side matter either, because guidance does not arrive without it, which is why the next words speak of guidance.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sorts doubt into two kinds. The claim itself may be false, or the listener may have misread it. The Book answers the first kind with an invitation to test it: (2:23 — **“And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true”**). The second kind is answered by teaching, because a person who doubts out of confusion needs the explanation this sūrah spends its length supplying. The same claim appears elsewhere in shorter form: (32:2 — **“The revelation of this Book is—beyond doubt—from the Lord of all worlds”**).

**WHY THE GUIDE GUIDES SOME READERS AND NOT OTHERS**

The verse ends by naming what the Book does and for whom, in the words ***“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. Al-Ṭabarī takes the word for guidance from its root: to show a man the road, point him to it and make it clear. He records al-Shaʿbī's gloss, guidance out of error, and the report of Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd that the Book is light for the mindful. Then he faces the question the wording raises. If the Book is guidance, why is it guidance for the mindful alone? His answer is worth reading twice. The Book is light for the mindful, a healing for the hearts of believers, a heaviness in the ears of those who deny it, blindness for those who refuse it, and a binding proof against those who reject it. One book, and different results, because the state of the reader is not the same.

Ibn Kathīr records how the early community defined the mindfulness the verse asks for. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asked Ubayy ibn Kaʿb what taqwā was, and Ubayy asked him whether he had ever walked a path lined with thorns. ʿUmar said he had. Ubayy asked what he did then. ʿUmar said he rolled up his sleeves and picked his way with care. Ubayy said, that is taqwā. Ibn Kathīr calls the root of the word the act of guarding yourself from what you dislike, and he supports the picture from the Qur'an: (90:10 — **“and shown them the two ways ˹of right and wrong˺”**).

Think of a lamp in a room. It does not choose who sees by it; it lights the room the same way for everyone. What decides whether a man finds the door is whether his eyes are open and whether he turned toward it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān counts three levels of guidance. Every creature receives the first level simply by being made and directed to what it needs. The second is offered to people who can choose, and the third is given to those who act on what they are shown. This verse promises the last two to the person who guards himself, as al-Saʿdī also reads the sentence.

---
## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**FAITH THAT STARTS WHERE THE EYES STOP**

The sūrah has described three kinds of people, and it turns to the first of them with the words ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”***. Al-Ṭabarī fills the phrase with reports, and his witnesses do not give one definition. Ibn ʿAbbās said the unseen is what comes from God, meaning the revelation itself. A report carried from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd, along with other Companions, names Paradise, the Fire, and everything God mentioned in the Qur'an, and adds a sharp observation: the believers among the Arabs did not take this belief from a book they already owned, or from knowledge they had inherited.

Zirr ibn Ḥubaysh, a Successor, said the unseen is the Qur'an. Qatādah listed Paradise, the Fire, the raising of the dead and the Day of Judgement. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas included God, His angels, His messengers, the Last Day with its garden and its fire, the meeting with Him, and life after death. Al-Ṭabarī then gives the root meaning that holds all of these together: the unseen is every thing hidden from you, and he settles on the reading that faith is belief with the heart, speech with the tongue and work with the limbs.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out why this phrase sits at the head of the list. If a man tells you the cloth in front of you is black and you agree, that is confirmation, not faith, because you agreed on what your own eyes handed you. Faith begins where the eyes stop. It is accepting a report with full trust in the one who gave it, without demanding to see it first. That is why the first thing the Book says about the people it guides is not how often they pray or how much they give. It is that they trust the unseen.

**A PRAYER THAT IS ESTABLISHED, NOT VISITED**

The verb in the verse is not do the prayer; it is establish it, and the early scholars explained the difference. Ibn ʿAbbās said it means to perform the prayer with everything that goes with it. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, reporting from him, said it means to complete the bowing, the prostration, the recitation, the humility and the attending. Qatādah said it means to guard the times, the washing, the bowing and the prostration.

Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān said it means to keep the time, finish the purity the prayer needs, and complete its bowing, prostration, recitation, witness and blessings. Al-Qurṭubī records the same concern from the early community in other words and ties it to the Book's own command elsewhere: (23:2 — **“those who are humble in their prayers”**). A body standing in prayer while the mind wanders through the day is doing something; establishing the prayer is another matter entirely.

**GIVING OUT OF WHAT IS IN THE HAND**

The third quality is ***“and donate from what We have provided for them”***, and the phrase begins with from, a preposition that carries weight. The Muslim gives out of what he holds, not the whole of it. The early commentators differed about which giving was meant first, and al-Ṭabarī sets out the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās called it the obligatory alms on wealth. A report from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions says it was a man's spending on his own family, before the law of alms came down.

Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, in a report Ibn Kathīr carries, said it meant giving freely according to what a person could manage, until the rules of charity arrived later in the Qur'an. Ibn Kathīr notices how often the Book joins prayer and giving in one breath, and gives the reason: prayer is the right of God, and giving is the right of His creatures, so the two together cover a person's duty in both directions.

The three qualities climb in a deliberate order, from the inward to the outward. Belief in the unseen is hidden inside a man, where no neighbour can see it. Prayer is half hidden and half visible: a body stands and bows, and only God knows what the heart brought. Giving leaves the pocket and enters the street, where the poor can see it and the ledger can record it. Think of a sailor steering by a star he cannot touch. He is not being foolish; the star has never failed anyone who steered by it. The believer steers his life by things he cannot see, and the two qualities that follow make that trust visible: a body that stands in prayer, and a hand that gives what it holds.

---
## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**FAITH THAT TAKES EVERY MESSENGER**

The fourth verse adds two qualities to the portrait, and the first is ***“and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the sense in a sentence: they believe in what Allah sent you with, and in what the earlier messengers were sent with; they do not split one messenger from another, and they reject nothing that came from their Lord. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same in fewer words: without distinction. A believer does not pick a favourite prophet and discard the rest, because the messages come from one sender and the messengers spoke for one Lord.

The Book states the same principle in the sūrah of the Women, where the command is to declare belief in God, in what was revealed to the Messenger, and in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the tribes, and to make no difference between any of them: (4:152 — **“And those who believe in Allah and His messengers and make no distinction between any of them—He will soon give them their rewards.”**). Faith in the last message carries faith in every message that led to it, which is why the community is asked to state both in one breath.

**THE SCRIPTURES THAT CAME BEFORE**

The second quality is ***“and what was revealed before you”***, and the line raises a question the commentators took seriously. Does the description continue the believers of the previous verse, or does it introduce a second group? Al-Ṭabarī carries a report from al-Suddī that the two groups are the believers among the Arabs and the believers among the People of the Book, who had followed the earlier scriptures and then accepted the Prophet ﷺ, and that the last verse of the passage gathers both.

Ibn Kathīr takes the wider reading, and the Book supports it. The believers are told elsewhere that the Qur'an confirms what came before it and guards it, so that the earlier revelation is not discarded but carried forward and protected: (5:48 — **“And We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth, confirming what came before it and as a guardian over it.”**). This is a community that owns a library rather than a single volume, and it reads the newest book in the light of the ones before it.

**CERTAINTY ABOUT THE LIFE TO COME**

The verse closes with ***“and have sure faith in the Hereafter”***, and the words add a quality the previous verses had left unstated. The Arabic carries more than belief: it carries a settled assurance that keeps a person steady, and Ibn Kathīr reads it as the firm knowledge of what is to come, joined to action. Al-Saʿdī explains the same sense, that the person described here expects the meeting with his Lord and prepares for it.

The Book gives the reason this certainty belongs at the head of the sūrah. It is the conviction that decides how a person spends what he has, because a man who believes that accounts are settled elsewhere can give in this world without fear: (2:3 — **“and donate from what We have provided for them”**). Al-Qurṭubī notes that the Hereafter is named in the Book with more than thirty names, and that the reason is the variety of its conditions, each name describing a different stage of the day, as al-Mukhtaṣar also observes.

The four qualities of these verses form one picture, and a reader can hold them in the order the Book gave them. A heart that trusts what it cannot see, a body that stands in prayer, a hand that gives from what it holds, and a mind that counts on the meeting with its Lord. Picture a man who sets out on a long sea voyage and packs for it. He carries a compass when he cannot see the coast, he keeps a routine on deck, he shares his supplies with the others on board, and he plans every day around the port he has never visited. The port is what makes the other three possible, because a man who expects no landfall has no reason to keep watch.

---
## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**THE POINTER THAT GATHERS UP THE THREE QUALITIES**

The passage closes with a pointer: those, not these. Al-Kashshāf and al-Bayḍāwī explain the work the pointer does. When the Book said earlier that it is guidance for the mindful, a listener could ask why the mindful were singled out for it. The three qualities that follow — trusting the unseen, prayer, giving — are the answer, and the pointer gathers them up: ***“It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord”***. Al-Bayḍāwī adds that the pointer is stronger than repeating the noun, because it throws the weight on the description and shows that the ruling follows from it.

Then comes the phrase that carries the promise, guidance from their Lord. Al-Bayḍāwī reads the Arabic preposition as a picture, the way a rider is upon an animal. Guidance is not something these people visit now and then; it is something they are settled on and carried by. And it comes from their Lord, not from their own cleverness. Ibn Kathīr calls it light, proof and insight, and al-Mukhtaṣar calls it the road that leads to Him.

**WHO THE PROMISE COVERS**

Al-Ṭabarī asks who the pointer covers, and his answer is generous. He reports that it gathers both groups described in the two earlier verses — the believers among the Arabs, and the believers among the People of the Book — and that the promise of guidance and success belongs to them together. Al-Ṭabarī also records two narrower readings: that the pointer covers only the mindful, or only the believers among the People of the Book. Then he returns to the grammar and shows why the argument can go either way. The words "and those who believe" can hang from the end of the first description or open a new sentence, and the closing verse settles the dispute by gathering everyone the passage has described into one promise. Difference of background does not divide the people of this Book, and the point is worth holding on to in a sūrah that will soon speak hard words to the Children of Israel.

**SUCCESS AS A FARMER'S WORD**

The second half of the verse names the outcome: ***“and it is they who will be successful”***. Al-Jalālayn spells success out plainly: they will enter Paradise and be saved from the Fire. Ibn Kathīr widens it to both lives, saying they gain what they were seeking and are kept from what they were dreading, in this world and the next. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps both halves: they gain what they desire, and they are saved from what they fear.

The Arabic word carries a picture that English leaves behind. Falāḥ means thriving and success, and the older language ties it to cultivation, to soil breaking open and a plant coming through. The call to prayer still uses the word, since after the call to prayer comes the call to falāḥ. So the sūrah's first portrait of a believer is a farmer's picture. He plants what no eye can see under the ground, and that is trust in the unseen. He waters it on time, and that is the prayer. He shares the crop, and that is the giving. The harvest is what the verse names.

The five verses just read are the measuring line of the sūrah, and the verses that follow show the people who fail it: those who reject the message outright, and those who talk like believers while living as strangers to the Book. Success in this sūrah is never a private mood or a feeling in the chest. It is a promise with a picture attached: a road that a person is settled on, a Lord who gave it, and a harvest waiting at the end of the season.

---
## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**THE SECOND GROUP, AND THE WORD THAT DESCRIBES THEM**

The sūrah has finished describing the believers, and it now turns to a second group: ***“As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not”***. The Arabic verb is kafara, and Ibn Kathīr gives its sense at the root — to cover something and hide it. A person who covers a truth he can see is not the same as a person who never met it.

The early scholars differed about who was meant, and al-Ṭabarī sets out the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās held that the verse came down about the Jews living near Madinah, who knew who the Prophet ﷺ was and still refused him. A second report from Ibn ʿAbbās, carried through ʿIkrimah and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, says that the opening of this sūrah, down to its hundredth verse, concerns named rabbis and named hypocrites from the tribes of Aws and Khazraj; al-Ṭabarī leaves the names out rather than lengthen his book with them. Al-Jalālayn lists a different pair at the other end of Arabia: Abū Jahl and Abū Lahab, the two men who fought the message hardest in Makkah. The descriptions fit all of them, which is why the sūrah does not name a single person.

**WHAT WARNING STILL DOES**

The clause about warning needs care, because it can be misread into carelessness. Al-Saʿdī explains what the warning still does. For a person whose rejection has settled into a fixed quality, the call no longer changes his ending, but it still establishes God's proof against him. The messenger is not a salesman who is judged by his sales. He is judged by whether he delivered the message, and the verse is spoken to comfort him at a moment when the rejection was personal and painful. Al-Saʿdī reads the verse as cutting off the Prophet's ﷺ longing to save every single person in front of him, and he points to the same comfort elsewhere: (36:10 — **“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”**).

The Qur'an repeats this sentence in more than one place, and the repetition has a purpose. Ibn Kathīr gathers the companions to this verse. (10:96 — **“Indeed, those against whom Allah’s decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe”**), and the verse after it, (10:97 — **“even if every sign were to come to them—until they see the painful punishment”**), describe people whom no argument will reach, because their refusal is not caused by a shortage of evidence. (63:6 — **“It is the same whether you pray for their forgiveness or not, Allah will not forgive them”**) carries the same shape into the case of forgiveness.

Think of a doctor who diagnoses a patient who has already stopped listening. He still writes the prescription and still records the visit. What he has done is put the truth in the patient's hand and on the record. The medicine will not be swallowed, but nobody can later say the doctor kept quiet.

**A VERDICT ABOUT A GROUP, NOT A LICENCE**

The verse ends with ***“they will never believe”***, and Al-Jalālayn reads the ending as a statement about God's knowledge: they do not believe, and God knows that they will not, so the Prophet ﷺ should not build his hopes on their turning. The point is not that God's word turned them away from faith. The whole sūrah treats the rejection as something they chose first, and the sealing that the next verse describes is described as a consequence of that choice. Ibn Kathīr keeps both halves of the matter together and warns against a reader who pulls them apart in either direction.

The grammar adds a small detail that the study draft in tafsir_initial notes. The words can be read as a present tense statement — they do not believe — or as a future, they will not believe. Arabic carries both, and the translation's choice of the future reads the sentence as a verdict about an outcome rather than a report about a moment. The verdict belongs to a particular group whose path God has disclosed, and it is not a rule that lets a reader write off any person he happens to find difficult. When the Prophet ﷺ entered Makkah years later, the men who had led the killing became Muslims, and Abū Sufyān and others of the same generation ended their lives as believers. The verse is about the ones whose refusal is final, and only God knows which case is which, as al-Ṭabarī also observes when he refuses to fix the verse on one set of names.

---
## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**THE PICTURE A SEAL MAKES**

The next verse names the consequence of the refusal in the plainest physical terms: ***“Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered.”*** Al-Ṭabarī opens with the shape of the word. The root of the seal means to stamp, and a stamp is pressed on a container. He then asks the question a reader wants to ask. How can a heart be sealed when a seal needs a lid or a wrapper? His answer builds the picture that makes the verse work. Hearts are containers for knowledge, and the hearing is the door by which news of the unseen arrives. Seal them shut and the contents stop changing.

He also records the disagreement about what the seal is, since the Qur'an leaves it unnamed. Al-Suddī says it means simply that God sealed them. Qatādah says the people obeyed Shayṭān first, and then God sealed the three faculties, so that none of them could reach the guidance again. Mujāhid describes it as a stamp that arrives when sin settles in a heart and surrounds it from every side. The sequence in all these reports is the same, and it runs in one direction. The sin comes first, and the seal follows. Nobody is described as sealed before he refused.

**SIN FIRST, THEN THE SEAL**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gathers the same point into a sentence worth remembering: one punishment for an evil deed is another evil deed that comes after it, and one reward for a good deed is another good deed that follows. He records the hadith that describes the process grown slow and visible: when a servant commits a sin a black dot appears on his heart, and each new sin adds another until the heart turns dark and can no longer see good as good. The report is carried in the Musnad of Aḥmad and the Sunan of At-Tirmidhī, as Maʿārif notes by way of Mishkāt al-Maṣābīh, and the Qur'an names that darkness: (83:14 — **“their hearts have been stained by all ˹the evil˺ they used to commit”**).

Al-Saʿdī draws the line that keeps the verse honest. The three roads of knowledge are closed by the sealing. The heart no longer takes in what would help it, the ear no longer benefits from what it hears, and the eye no longer sees what would steer it. This is a punishment, and the punishment fits the crime, because these are the same three faculties that were offered the truth and refused it. The Qur'an states the rule in one line: (6:110 — **“We turn their hearts and eyes away ˹from the truth˺ as they refused to believe at first”**).

A man who bolts every door of his house at night and then complains that no light comes in has described his own situation accurately, and this is the shape the verse draws. The doors were open while the message was being offered. What closes them is the refusal itself.

**THE PENALTY THE VERSE CLOSES ON**

The verse ends with the sentence the earlier one withheld: ***“They will suffer a tremendous punishment”***. Al-Jalālayn describes the punishment as intense and lasting, and the Arabic word carries weight rather than merely size. Notice which faculties are named in the first half and which group they belong to. The open rejecters lose the heart, the hearing and the sight — the three instruments a person needs to find the road — and then they meet a penalty that is called tremendous.

The sūrah keeps a careful vocabulary for its two groups, and the difference is worth noticing as the verses go by. The rejecters are promised a tremendous punishment, and the liars of the next passage are promised a painful one. The Book never uses the same word twice where a reader might learn something from the change. The end of the passage returns a third time with a different word again, as al-Qurṭubī notes when he compares the three.

---
## Verse 2:8

> And there are some who say, “We believe in Allah and the Last Day,” yet they are not ˹true˺ believers

**THE THIRD GROUP ARRIVES WITHOUT A NAME**

***“And there are some who say,”*** the verse begins, and the sūrah turns to its third group. It does not name them; it only says that among people there are some who talk, and the verses that follow describe them until the name arrives at 2:14. Ibn Kathīr defines the condition in one line: nifāq is to show agreement and conceal something evil. Ibn Jurayj, quoted in his commentary, gives a portrait of the person this describes. His deeds differ from what he advertises, what he hides differs from what he says, and his entrance is not like his exit.

The historical setting explains why these verses belong to Madinah and not to Makkah. Ibn Kathīr lays the story out. In Makkah there was almost the opposite problem: some people were forced to hide their faith while their hearts held it. Madinah was different. Two Arab tribes, Aws and Khazraj, had lived there in the days before Islam, alongside three Jewish tribes, and the two Arab tribes were on the edge of making ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl their king when the message arrived and much of the city accepted Islam. After Badr, when the Muslims became strong enough to be worth reckoning with, he and others like him entered Islam with their tongues while their hearts stayed where they were. Fear, not conviction, wrote the sentence on their lips.

Al-Qurṭubī records how neatly the sūrah divides its opening peoples. Mujāhid counted four verses about the believers, two about the rejecters, and thirteen about the hypocrites, and al-Qurṭubī agrees with the count. He also records a remark of the early teachers that the word for people here is a general noun, and that the friends of God are not described with it. The sūrah spends more time on the pretenders than on the open enemies, and the length is the lesson. The community was going to live with these people, marry into their families and trade with them, so it needed to know how to read them.

**THE HALF-TRUTH THEY CHOSE**

Their statement is a half-truth, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān picks up exactly what is missing from it, in the words ***“We believe in Allah and the Last Day”***. The men claiming to believe mention Allah and the Last Day, and the name of the Prophet ﷺ does not appear in their sentence at all. Even a group this hostile would rather avoid a specific lie than tell it. Faith in the Messenger would have been a claim about a man standing in front of them, and something in them hesitated over that. So they built a formula broad enough to sound right and vague enough to be safe, and the Book reports the same instinct in another place: (63:1 — **“When the hypocrites come to you ˹O Prophet˺, they say, ‘We bear witness that you are certainly the Messenger of Allah’”**). Al-Qurṭubī reads that later formula as the same kind of edit, a sentence chosen for what it does not say.

The sūrah gathers this kind of speech in more than one place, and the pattern repeats. (49:14 — **“˹Some of˺ the nomadic Arabs say, ‘We believe.’ Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘You have not believed. But say, “We have submitted,” for faith has not yet entered your hearts.”**), where the answer separates the word from the state with one correction. The Book draws a line here that a reader should hold on to. A claim of faith is not itself faith, and the community is asked to look past the sentence to the life underneath it.

**A CLAIM THAT THE VERSE REFUSES**

The verse closes by refusing the claim outright: ***“yet they are not ˹true˺ believers”***. The Arabic states the denial with a construction that leaves no room for a polite reading, and the translation's brackets show it. They say one thing, and the verse says they are not believers at all. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the plain sense: some people claim faith in Allah when they do not in fact believe.

Al-Saʿdī divides the condition into two kinds so a reader can tell them apart. There is hypocrisy in belief, which removes a person from Islam altogether, and it is the condition of the men this passage is about. There is also hypocrisy in deed, which is a serious sin but leaves a person inside the community. For the second kind he describes the report the Prophet ﷺ gave about such persons: when they speak they lie, when they promise they fail, and when they are trusted they betray; in another narration, when they argue they abuse. Al-Saʿdī's care with the two categories matters, because it stops a Muslim from throwing the heaviest word in the language at anyone who disappoints him.

Both kinds share one structure, and it is worth drawing. The outside and the inside do not match. Picture a shop hanging a sign that says genuine goods, while its back room holds something else. The sign is real, the letters are painted, and the contents are unchanged by any of it. The verse is about the gap between the sign and the room, and the next verses describe what the gap costs.

---
## Verse 2:9

> They seek to deceive Allah and the believers, yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it

**THE SAFETY THEY THOUGHT THEY BOUGHT**

The verse names the offence in its own words first: ***“They seek to deceive Allah”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the deception in practical terms. The hypocrite shows with his tongue what is not in his heart, and he does it to escape the rulings that fall on a declared rejecter — the sword, capture, and the loss of property. He is buying safety with a sentence, and he thinks the purchase is complete.

Al-Qurṭubī collects the explanations the scholars gave for how deceiving Allah is even possible in a sentence. The first reading says the words describe his intent as he imagines it: he is acting like a man who thinks he can cheat. A second reading says the sentence describes his deeds rather than his thoughts, the way a deed is named for what it resembles. A third reading, which al-Qurṭubī traces to al-Ḥasan and others, holds that a word has been left out and the sense is that they deceive the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, and that deceiving the Messenger is deceiving the One who sent him. Al-Qurṭubī also preserves a linguistic note from Thaʿlab, taken from Ibn al-Aʿrābī, that the root of this word in Arabic carries the sense of corruption.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson out of the elision. No man in that city ever believed he had outsmarted God. What they actually did was try to deceive the Prophet ﷺ and the Muslims, and the verse elevates the offence by naming the One whose authority stood behind them. Putting a hand on a prophet or on the community that carries his message is a hand raised at the One who sent them both. That is the sense of the verse, and it is the same line the Qur'an takes when it warns about mocking the religion or hurting its Messenger.

**THE DECEPTION THAT LANDS AT HOME**

The sentence then reaches for the community: ***“and the believers,”*** and it turns the whole matter around with ***“yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it”***. The sentence turns the whole matter around. The deception lands at home. Al-Mukhtaṣar explains what they gave up for it: they convinced nobody in a position to help them, and Allah knows every secret and has already told the believers what they are dealing with. A private lie told in a public hall is now printed in a Book that is read aloud in every mosque.

The Qur'an states the reversal of this equation in a verse that fills out the picture: (4:142 — **“Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He outwits them”**). The same reference appears in tafsir_initial's note on this verse, with a list of places where the plotting of the disbelievers meets a counter-plan it never saw coming (10:21, 13:42, 14:46, 16:26, 27:50).

Al-Qushayrī draws the same turn of events with an image the Qur'an uses elsewhere. A traveller in the desert sees what looks like water in the distance and walks toward it, and when he reaches the spot he finds nothing there but the account he owes: (24:39 — **“their deeds are like a mirage in a desert, which the thirsty perceive as water, but when they approach it, they find it to be nothing”**). Nobody cheated that traveller. His own eyes did, and the Qur'an hands the picture to the man who thinks a sentence has bought him safety. One commentary in the sources presses the same reading with a modern comparison: hypocrisy in a heart like this behaves the way a cancer behaves, growing while the patient feels well, and the deal that looked profitable is running at a loss.

The last words explain why a man keeps doing this to himself: he fails to perceive it. The next verses use the same language twice more, and the effect is a portrait of a person who is the last one to understand his own position. Picture a man in a crowded market who hides his money in a torn coat so nobody can steal it. He is the only person in that market who does not know that the cold comes in through the hole.

---
## Verse 2:10

> There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase. They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies

**A DIAGNOSIS THAT GOES TO THE FOUNDATION**

The verse opens with a diagnosis in five words: ***“There is sickness in their hearts,”***. Al-Ṭabarī begins from the word itself. Sickness is the opposite of health when it comes to bodies, and the word stretches to cover the state of a person's religion as well. He then explains why the Qur'an calls the problem a heart problem and leaves the rest unsaid. Speaking of a sickness in the heart is enough to convey that the sickness is in the beliefs the heart holds, without dragging their private thoughts into the open. The Book names the location, and the location carries the meaning.

The early commentators were nearly of one voice about what the illness is. Ibn Kathīr gathers them: Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and their colleagues described it as doubt, and Mujāhid, ʿIkrimah, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, Abū al-ʿĀliyah, al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas and Qatādah said the same. Ibn Zayd added a clarifying line his commentary preserves: it is a disease in religion, not a disease of the body. Al-Qurṭubī quotes the linguist Ibn Fāris with a definition wide enough to cover the whole verse: sickness is anything that carries a man out of the boundary of soundness, whether that is an illness, hypocrisy, or a shortfall in some matter.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān works the medical picture through. In the body, illness is a loss of balance, so the parts no longer work together and the whole system can fail. Applied to the inner life, the same loss of balance keeps a person from the health of the spirit, and it does its damage quietly, at the root, far from where people are watching.

A doctor looking at a failing building would not start with the windows. He would check the foundation for water damage, because the visible cracks are the symptoms and the water is the cause. This verse goes straight to that depth and puts the diagnosis in one word: sick.

**DOUBT GIVEN A PERMANENT ROOM**

The next clause adds the increase: ***“and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase.”*** Al-Qurṭubī records how the scholars read this increase. Some read the sentence as a prayer against them, and the wording does have the shape of a wish. Others read it as a decree of consequence: doubt is added to doubt and hypocrisy to hypocrisy, so that what began as hesitation becomes a settled position, and every step down the road makes the next step easier. The Qur'an adds hesitation to their hesitation only after they have chosen the road themselves.

Al-Saʿdī puts the accounting side by side: the increase is a punishment shaped like the crime. A man who treated doubt as a guest does not get it removed at his convenience; he gets the guest given a permanent room. And this increase is not a foreign invasion of a healthy heart. It is the natural growth of a seed the owner planted and kept watering.

The Qur'an describes the same principle outside this sūrah, in a warning that applies to everyone: (74:31 — **“We have made their number only as a test for the disbelievers, so that the People of the Book will be certain, and the believers will increase in faith.”**) Notice the verb that appears in both verses. The same disclosure that grows a believer's faith grows the doubter's disease, and the difference is not in what arrived but in what was waiting to receive it.

Think of land beside a river. Where the water finds good soil it feeds the field, and where the same water finds a broken levee it widens the gap. The river has not changed direction; the ground decided the outcome of that season. The verse describes what a heart does with what reaches it.

**THE PUNISHMENT THAT NAMES THE LIE**

The sentence closes with ***“They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies”***, and the reason it gives is the lie itself. The last clause gives the reason the punishment is theirs, and it names the specific offence: lying. tafsir_initial records a reading held by the scholars, that the Arabic word can be read as they deny rather than they lie, and it reports al-Ṭabarī's reason for preferring lying: the same verdict is passed on the hypocrites at the opening of Sūrah al-Munāfiqūn, where the Qur'an says plainly that they are liars. The word for the pain here is the one the sūrah uses for a wound that is felt — the same word family as the pain of an injury — which is the right note for men whose offence was carried out in sound and words rather than with hands.

Put the two ends of the passage side by side and the sūrah's teaching about honesty comes out. The open rejecter, who shouts his refusal in the street, is met with a tremendous punishment. The man who lies quietly while holding the same refusal is met with a painful one, and the verse names the lie as the reason. There is no special mercy for words: from the point of view of this Book, the sentence a man says and the thing he actually does have to answer to each other. A person may lie to a neighbour's face, but the act under the lie still stands in the record, and this is the record the verse is talking about. A witness who signs a false statement does not change the crash he is describing. He only adds a second crime to the first.

---
## Verse 2:11

> When they are told, “Do not spread corruption in the land,” they reply, “We are only peace-makers!”

**THE CORRECTION THEY WERE GIVEN TO THEIR FACES**

The correction is reported in the verse's own words: ***“When they are told,”*** ***“Do not spread corruption in the land,”*** The next three verses show the same men being corrected to their faces. Al-Suddī reports from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd that the people being addressed are the hypocrites, and that the corruption forbidden here is called out in their own terms: rejection of God and acts of disobedience. Al-Saʿdī widens the list in a way that makes the charge concrete. Corruption includes carrying the believers' private news to their enemies and giving loyalty to men who were at war with the community. al-Baghawī notes that some of the scholars read the address as being aimed at the Jews around Madinah instead, and marks the two readings without choosing between them.

The word for the land here is the ordinary one, and the scholars were not fussy about it. The command concerns the ground a person walks on and the streets of the town he lives in; the harm a community suffers is named in the language of the earth it stands on. The instruction is not a philosophical warning about abstract evil. It is a neighbour telling another neighbour to stop breaking things where people live.

Al-Baghawī gives both readings of who was being addressed and both reasons for the command. The instruction was said to the hypocrites, and some of the scholars said it was said to the Jews, delivered by the believers. The corruption it forbids is named in their words: rejection of God and holding people back from faith in Muḥammad ﷺ and the Qur'an. He adds a plain reading that lands the whole matter in one sentence: the command means do not disbelieve, and rejection is the deepest corruption of religion, because it breaks the one relationship everything else stands on. Al-Qurṭubī adds a grammatical detail that shows how common this exchange was — the little word for when marks an event that repeats, so the scene is not a single conversation but an argument the believers kept having with them.

Al-Ṭabarī records a remark from the other end of the matter, one worth keeping in view in a sūrah full of specific people. Salmān al-Fārisī said that these people had not yet arrived — meaning that the description of the hypocrite would keep producing fresh examples in every generation. The faces change and the conduct returns, and the sūrah was written to be useful to the later ones.

**THE SMALL WORD THAT SHRINKS AN ACT**

Their answer is given in the same verse: ***“they reply,”*** ***“We are only peace-makers!”*** Their answer uses a small word with a large job. In Arabic, innamā means only or nothing but, and al-Saʿdī points out what happens when it is used in a claim like this. The sentence does not merely say that they are reformers; it locks the title to their own side and, in the same breath, implies that the men telling them to stop belong to nobody. An answer that sounds humble is carrying an accusation, and the verse records it without correction for one full verse, letting the reader hear how such a sentence lands.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān opens up what is wrong with the reply at the level of evidence. Whether a man is a maker of peace or a maker of disorder is not settled by the label he chooses for himself. It is settled by what his actions produce. He gives the useful example: what thief describes himself as a thief? The claim is the easiest part of any man's life to manufacture, and it costs him nothing, which is exactly why the sūrah refuses to weigh it.

Think of a driver who cuts across three lanes and then leans on his horn to explain himself. Everyone he passed has a different account of what happened, and the road, in the end, keeps the only record that counts. The claim in the verse is loud. The tracks of the cars behind it are louder.

---
## Verse 2:12

> Indeed, it is they who are the corruptors, but they fail to perceive it

**THE ANSWER THAT REVERSES THE CHARGE**

The answer begins with the sūrah's own verdict: ***“Indeed, it is they”*** ***“who are the corruptors,”*** God answers the claim without softening it. Al-Ṭabarī explains the shape of the reply: the men had been instructed to obey and forbidden to disobey, and they had answered that they were the reformers and their instructors were the ones in error. The verse turns that around with a sentence built for the reversal. They are the ones who opposed God's command, crossed His limits, rode over what He forbade and abandoned what He required — and they are not the Muslims who were telling them to stop.

Al-Saʿdī presses the point further than a reader expects, and the passage is one of the strongest in the sūrah. He asks whether any corruption can be greater than rejecting the signs of God, blocking the road to Him, treating Him and His friends as a man deceives an enemy, and holding loyalty with those who fought Him — and then giving that whole position the name of repair. His conclusion is the verse's conclusion: no corruption stands further from peace than a corruption that calls itself reform.

Al-Qurṭubī notices the small word of warning at the front of the sentence and the emphasis that follows it, and he draws the obvious lesson from the claim and the ruling taken together: the man who puts forward big claims is often the one whose condition is the opposite of what he says. The verse is a translation key for the rest of the sūrah. Every time the reader meets a claim in these verses, the next verse hands over the reality it was covering.

**WHY THEY COULD NOT SEE IT**

And the verse ends with the words the hypocrites cannot hear: ***“but they fail to perceive it”***. Al-Baghawī pauses over the two-word alarm that opens this verse, a word the Arabs used to make a listener sit up before the sentence landed, and al-Qurṭubī lists what the grammarians did with the emphasis that follows it. He also preserves a question and two answers from Ibn Kaysān. The question is fair: what blame can fall on a man who truly does not know that he is a corrupter? The first answer is that they were doing their damage in secret and wearing the mask of repair in public, and what they did not perceive was that their affairs would be laid open before the Prophet ﷺ and read out in a Book recited to the end of time. The second answer is that they did not perceive the punishment God had stored up for them. Either way, the man's ignorance is not an excuse here; it is one more thing he is being told.

The sentence ends the way the earlier one ended, and the repetition is a signal. These men cannot see the hole they are standing in, and the reason is not an absence of information. The community had already explained the matter to them face to face; that is what the whole exchange in the verse records. What is missing is perception, and the Arabic word used here describes a man who bumps into the truth and blames the wall.

Track the words the sūrah uses for this blindness as it moves. The men who tried to cheat their way into safety fail to perceive it. The men who call their neighbours corrupt and themselves the repairers fail to perceive it a second time. When the sūrah reaches the next verse the word changes: they do not know. The Book walks a person down a path, and the vocabulary narrows with each step. Failing to perceive the truth can end in failing to know it at all, and the difference between the two words is the distance between a man who has not looked and a man who can no longer see. Think of a broken lighthouse. It is a nuisance. A lighthouse whose lamp has been replaced by a painted stone is a trap, and this verse is written for readers who need to tell the difference.

---
## Verse 2:13

> And when they are told, “Believe as others believe,” they reply, “Will we believe as the fools believe?” Indeed, it is they who are fools, but they do not know

**A STANDARD INSTEAD OF A PROHIBITION**

The instruction is quoted as it was given: ***“And when they are told,”*** ***“Believe”*** ***“as others believe,”*** The instruction comes a third time, and this time it names a standard instead of a prohibition. Al-Ṭabarī explains what the men were being asked to accept: to believe in the Messenger and in what was sent down with him, along with what follows from acceptance — the resurrection, the standing, the reckoning, and the world after death. He records the report from Ibn ʿAbbās through al-Ḍaḥḥāk describing the content of the instruction in the plainest terms: say that he is a prophet and a messenger, and that what was revealed with him is true.

The word translated as others is al-nās, the people, and the scholar are agreed about who it meant in Madinah. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān all record the identification of the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ — the men who had left their homes and accepted a Book that was still coming down. Al-Qurṭubī spells out the grammar of the instruction, reading the comparison as attached to a deleted noun: believe with a faith that is like the faith of those people.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the rule out of the instruction to a community that would live long after that generation. If the faith of the Companions is the standard the Qur'an sets, then the shape of Islam as they practiced it is not a historical curiosity; it is the reference point against which later claims are checked. The verse does something practical with the leaders of that generation. It makes them the measurement, and it makes the man who refuses to be measured by anything but himself a man who has already answered his own question.

**AN ANSWER THAT WAS NOT AN ARGUMENT**

The answer comes back with contempt in it: ***“they reply,”*** ***“Will we believe”*** ***“as the fools believe?”*** The men did not answer with an argument. They answered with contempt, and the form of it is worth seeing. The question at the front of their sentence is not a request for information, and the word at its end is one of the heaviest insults in the language of that time. Ibn Kathīr records the identification that the commentators of the first generations gave: by the fools they meant the Companions of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. The men who had just been invited to believe alongside the heroes of Badr answered by putting the whole community in a single sack and tying it shut.

Al-Qurṭubī adds the detail that explains why the community is told about this at all. The men did not say such things openly. The sentence was said in private, with mockery in it, and God informed His Prophet ﷺ about what they were concealing. tafsir_initial preserves a related observation from the commentators: some of these men knew perfectly well that the believers were the intelligent ones, and the label was chosen to make themselves look important. An insult used as a tool is still an insult, and the record shows the tool.

**THE DEFINITION THAT SETTLES THE LABEL**

The Book answers the label itself: ***“Indeed, it is they”*** ***“who are fools, but they do not know”***. The answer reverses the label, and al-Saʿdī defines the terms so the reversal can be tested rather than taken on trust. Foolishness, in his words, is a man's ignorance of his own interest and his efforts spent on what harms him. The opposite quality, sound reason, is knowing where your interest lies, working for what benefits you and warding off what harms you. Then he lays the two groups against that standard. The Companions left their homes, separated from their clans and stood against the strongest men of Arabia to hold on to a message they were sure of. The men calling them fools had already given up their own inner convictions to save their skins. Against the definition they themselves were using, the labels belong to the people they were pointing at.

He closes with the sentence that makes the verse unforgettable: the matter is judged by descriptions and proofs, not by claims and empty words. That is the rule by which the whole passage can be read. Every group in these opening verses states its case. The proof of what each one is stands outside the statement, in the way the person lives, and the reader is being taught to keep his eye on the second thing.

A man at the seafront laughs at a neighbour who spends his money on a lifejacket, then watches the weather turn from the shore while the other one is on the water. The laughter was cheap, and the cost of being wrong was not. This verse puts the laughing man and the man in the jacket side by side, and asks the reader which of the two he would rather be when the wind rises.

---
## Verse 2:14

> When they meet the believers they say, “We believe.” But when alone with their evil associates they say, “We are definitely with you; we were only mocking.”

**ONE DAY, TWO CONVERSATIONS**

The first half of the day is described in the verse: ***“When they meet the believers they say,”*** ***“We believe.”*** Ibn Kathīr explains what the men were after. When they met the believers they declared faith and acted like loyal friends. The point was to misdirect them and to keep a share of whatever the Muslims gained. Their sentence was not a confession of belief. It was a subscription to a group that was going somewhere, bought cheaply and held loosely.

Al-Baghawī names the people they were meeting: the emigrants from Makkah and the helpers of Madinah, the two groups whose names the whole city knew. Their greeting was a claim of sameness: we believe as you believe. tafsir_initial records the occasion the commentators give for the verse. A hypocrite told his companions to watch how he would turn these fools away from them. He then went to some of the Companions, praised them warmly, and came back with one instruction for his own people: when you see them, do as I do. The Companions reported the whole scene to the Prophet ﷺ, and the verse came down and laid the man's method open.

Al-Qurṭubī adds a small note on the shape of the first word, which tells a reader how ordinary these meetings were. The verb for meeting has swallowed a letter to make it easier on the tongue, and a variant reading in the sources restores it. The community's neighbours were dropping in and out of each other's rooms every day. This was not a dispute between distant camps; it was a problem inside a town where everyone lived on top of everyone else.

**THE COMPANY THEY WENT BACK TO**

The second half is drawn just as plainly: ***“But when alone with their evil associates they say,”*** The sūrah gives the other half of the same day. Ibn Kathīr explains the word translated as associates: it means their devils, and he quotes al-Ṭabarī on who those were. Every kind of creature has its devils, the mischievous ones inside it, and the devils of human beings are human too. Their associates here were the leaders and masters among the rabbis, the hypocrites and the idol worshippers — the people whose approval they actually wanted. Ibn Kathīr points to the Qur'an's own statement of that rule: (6:112 — **“We have made for every prophet an enemy—devils from among humans and jinn”**).

Ibn ʿAbbās, in the reports carried by al-Baghawī and in his own commentary, goes further and gives five names. Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf among the Jews of Madinah, Abū Burdah al-Aslamī among the tribe of Aslam, Ibn al-Sawdāʾ across the northern border in Syria, ʿAbd al-Dār among Juhaynah, and ʿAwf ibn ʿĀmir among the tribe of ʿĀmir. Whether or not every name is settled, the picture is clear: a network of men spread across the region, each holding influence in his own area, each hearing the same confidential reports.

Al-Qurṭubī pauses over the grammar of the sentence, because the ordinary Arabic for being alone with someone uses a different preposition. He answers that the verb here means they went off and turned back to them, so the scene is not merely two men in a room. It is a man walking away from the mosque and heading home to a different loyalty. The direction of travel is part of the meaning.

**TWO SENTENCES FOR TWO AUDIENCES**

Their own sentence is then quoted: ***“We are definitely with you”*** ***“we were only mocking.”*** The two halves of their sentence are doing different work. Al-Jalālayn reads the first as a pledge about religion: we are with you, we follow what you follow, in secret. That is the sentence a man says when he wants to be counted on the inside. Al-Mukhtaṣar states the same in plainer words: we are really with you, and we only make fun of them when we say that we believe.

The second half repeats a device the sūrah has already used. The little word that means only appears here for the third time in a few verses: we are only peace-makers, we were only mocking. In each case a man uses the word to shrink his own act and to place the blame on the people across from him. Notice also how neatly the two halves cover the two audiences. To the believers they offer a claim about what they believe. To their own people they offer a claim about what they were doing. Neither sentence is about the truth of the matter, which is exactly what the Qur'an is exposing.

Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators record that the mockery had a target: the Prophet ﷺ and his Companions. Think of a man who laughs at the people of a village from inside their own guest room, then goes back to his own street and repeats the joke to a crowd that enjoys it. He believes he has handled both sides. The verse ends with his own words in his mouth, and the next verse shows him beginning to pay for them.

---
## Verse 2:15

> Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance

**MOCKERY THAT IS THROWN BACK**

The verse states what happens to the mockery they threw: ***“Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance”***. The sentence needs care, because it speaks about God in the language of the men He is answering. Al-Qurṭubī reports the position of the majority of the scholars: what is meant is that God takes vengeance on them and punishes them, and He answers their mockery with a penalty, so the punishment is given the name of the offence. The Arabs used this pattern constantly, he says. Their poets called a man's retaliation his ignorance, though no sane man boasts of ignorance, because the two words were set opposite each other and the sentence stayed balanced. The Qur'an follows the same usage: (42:40 — **“the recompense of an evil deed is an evil like it”**), and (2:194 — **“whoever violates ˹the sacred limits˺ against you, then violate ˹them˺ against him”**), where the deed and the answer carry the same name and not the same nature.

tafsir_initial sets out the two ways the commentators handle the difficulty. Some say the disbelievers bring the ridicule on themselves as a fair return, and that no mocking starts with God. Others, following al-Rāzī, say that the disgrace and the humiliation with which God leaves them is itself the answer to their mockery, and the Qur'an calls that answer mocking because that is what it feels like from where they stand. Ibn ʿAbbās, in one report, describes the scene in the world to come: a gate of Paradise is opened for them and then shut in their faces, and the believers who had been their joke get the last look.

**THE ANSWER THE MOCKERY GOT**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the practical lesson out of the same words. God gave these men a long rope in His forbearance and mercy. They took the absence of punishment as proof that nothing was watching, and their confidence grew while the rope grew short. When the cup of their iniquity was full, they were caught, and the very respite they had been enjoying turned out to be the arrangement of their downfall. The same teaching appears in the sūrah later, where the delay is named as a favour that will end: (2:15), and (3:178 — **“the disbelievers should not think that it is better for them that We give them more time”**).

Al-Mukhtaṣar gathers the practical outcome of the sentence in a few lines. In this world the men are treated as believers, with all the protection that comes with the name, and their own account will be settled in the next one. The mockery they aimed at the believers is answered in the same currency, measure for measure, and the delay itself is part of the answer, because it lets them keep adding to the record they will be handed. Ibn Kathīr makes the same point about the word order of the verse: God answers the offence with its own name, so the men who turned a serious matter into a joke are dealt with in the language they chose.

The verse closes with a picture worth holding. Al-Jalālayn reads the last words as a state of wandering and hesitation, as men walking in circles in the dark. The root of the word in tafsir_initial means to cross a boundary, and the Qur'an uses it for the disbelievers in general and for Pharaoh in particular ((20:24; 79:17)), and repeats the phrase in other places ((6:110; 7:186; 10:11; 23:75)). A man who steps over a line in daylight knows what he is doing. A man who keeps stepping over lines in the dark is described by this verse, and the picture belongs to everyone who has ever mocked a truth and then kept walking.

---
## Verse 2:16

> They are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance. But this trade is profitless, and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided

**THE PEOPLE THE NEXT VERSES EXPLAIN**

The little phrase at the front is a signpost. It points back at every description the sūrah has given in the last three verses — the double speech, the mockery, the wandering in the dark — and says that the men who did all that are the people named in what comes next.

The verse begins with a pointer back at the men it has just described: ***“They are the ones”*** Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the link with the verse before it and says plainly who they turned out to be: these are the fools. That word has already been used in this sūrah, when the same men called the believers fools and the verse answered them. The sūrah now explains why the answer was right: the men had looked at two roads and taken the worse one, and they had done it as a business decision.

**WHAT THEY TRADED AND WHAT THEY GOT**

and then states the bargain they made: ***“who trade guidance for misguidance.”*** The scholars of the first generations gave slightly different pictures of the same exchange. Al-Suddī reports from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd that the men pursued misguidance and abandoned guidance, which is how the words work when a person walks one way and leaves the other behind. Mujāhid said they believed and then disbelieved, resting the verse on the moment the change happened. Qatādah said they preferred deviation to guidance, and Ibn Kathīr notes that the Qur'an says the same about an earlier people: (41:17 — **“as for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness over guidance”**).

That last reading is why the commentators read the verb, which normally means to buy, as a matter of preference. tafsir_initial explains that in Arabic this family of words covers choosing and swapping as well as purchasing, so a man who prefers one thing to another has, in the language of the market, bought it. The verse keeps the market word on purpose. The Book uses the same comparison in several places ((2:86; 2:175; 3:177)), and each time the point is the same: the men were not confused about the price. They knew what they were handing over.

**A TRADE THAT CARRIED NO PROFIT**

The sentence that follows weighs it: ***“But this trade is profitless”*** Al-Jalālayn spells out the loss: their commerce gained them nothing, and in fact it took from them, and the destination they had bought with it was a fire that does not end. Ibn ʿAbbās says the bargain did not prosper. Al-Qurṭubī adds that the word for trade is used here because trade is what the men thought they were doing, and a bad deal is best judged by naming it clearly.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān makes the sharpest point of the verse. In giving this action the name of trade, the Qur'an suggests that these worldly-wise men understood nothing about trade at all. A merchant checks the goods before he pays, keeps his capital, and knows that a profit must come back bigger than what he put in. These men handed over faith, the one thing that cannot be replaced, and received a road that ends in loss. He calls them men who could not even do the arithmetic of their own interest.

Kashānī, writing from the perspective of the inner life, gives the same accounting in another language. The capital a person holds is the light placed in him at his creation, and it was meant to be invested in knowledge, good work and character until it returned with profit. When that capital is traded for the darkness of appetite, nothing is left to show at the end but the empty hand.

Picture a farmer in the planting season who sells his seed grain for sweets and eats them the same evening. Nobody stole from him. He weighed the two things himself, and he chose the sweet thing over the field.

**THE ROAD THEY WERE NOT SHOWN**

and names what is left when the deal is done: ***“and they are not ˹rightly˺ guided”***. The verse ends by naming what remains after the deal is done. Al-Mukhtaṣar says they lost their faith in God and lost the way to the truth in one transaction. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the ending as the refusal of any rescue: they did not prosper in the bargain, and they were not guided out of the error they had entered.

The sūrah has said something like this three times now in slightly different words. The liars deceive nobody but themselves and do not perceive it. The men who call themselves peace-makers are corrupters and do not perceive it. The men who mocked the believers are trading guidance for misguidance and are not guided. Each sentence takes the account a little further from a mistake and a little closer to a settled condition. The Book is teaching its reader to notice the movement.

---
## Verse 2:17

> Their example is that of someone who kindles a fire, but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness—unable to see

**WHY THE SŪRAH TURNS TO PARABLES**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains why the sūrah changes its voice at this point. The last four verses of this opening passage do not describe the hypocrites any further; they show them, using two long comparisons. The first comparison is built on fire. The second, which begins a few verses later, is built on water. The two pictures are not decoration. They sort the hypocrites into the two kinds of people the Book has been describing.

The sūrah now sets the men beside a picture of its own making: ***“Their example is that of someone”*** In the first picture, the disbelief has gone deep. The man has almost no leaning toward Islam, and he carries the name of a Muslim only to keep the goods that come with it. In the second, the man recognises the truth, and sometimes wants to be a real Muslim, but his worldly interests hold him back, so he lives in permanent hesitation between the two. The fire belongs to the first man. The thunderstorm belongs to the second. What both share is the darkness they end up in.

**THE FIRE THAT LIT THE GROUND**

who strikes a light in the dark: ***“who kindles a fire”*** Ibn ʿAbbās, in his commentary, gives the scene its practical shape: a man lights a fire in the darkness to protect his family, his property and himself. That is what a fire was for in a world without streetlights and with wild animals moving in the night. He is not warming his hands for comfort. He is holding a wall of light between the people he loves and the dangers around them.

Kashānī reads the fire as the light of readiness in a person — the capacity to learn and to follow the truth, which flares for a while and then goes out. Whatever shape the reader gives the picture, the important detail is who lit it. This is a fire a man set going himself, and that matters for what happens next, because the sūrah is careful about what is chosen by the person and what is given by God.

**THE LIGHT TAKEN BACK**

and then loses it: ***“but when it lights up all around them, Allah takes away their light, leaving them in complete darkness”*** Al-Jalālayn notes a grammatical turn that carries meaning: the verse begins with one man and continues with the plural pronoun their. One man lit the fire, and the light belongs to a group. The commentators read the group as the people of his kind, the men who took the same bargain. They had a moment of safety from being counted as believers — the protection a man's public faith gave him in that city — and then the light went out.

Al-Durr al-Manthūr preserves the report of Ibn ʿAbbās on what the light stood for. It was the honour the hypocrites took from Islam, in the years when the name was worth having. The Muslims married into their families, inherited from them and shared the spoils of war with them, exactly as the rules of the community required. When the hypocrites died, God stripped them of that honour just as the fire-lighter was stripped of his light. Ibn ʿAbbās adds the explanation of the next word: the darkness is punishment.

tafsir_initial gathers the same identification: the light is the declaration of faith, the word that gave the hypocrite the right to eat with the community, to marry inside it and to claim its protection, and it is taken away at the moment when the truth can no longer be worked around. Ibn ʿAbbās also notes that some of the commentators applied the comparison to the Jews of Madinah, who knew the signs and did not carry them. Two of the reports reach the same end: the light was never theirs, and one day the arrangement ended.

Kashānī describes the process from the inside. The flare is the man's own capacity for guidance, shown in his interest in the world he can see; the going out is the slow fading of that capacity as he keeps choosing what is far away over what is near. Picture a traveller who lights a torch in a cave and studies the walls for a while, then watches the flame die and the walls disappear. He had enough light to find his way out. He did not use it, and the darkness that follows is not the same as the darkness an hour before. Now he knows what he is missing.

**LEFT IN THE DARK WITHOUT A ROAD**

so that they are left ***“unable to see”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the phrase as men in a state of confusion and fear, unable to see the ground they are standing on. Al-Mukhtaṣar says simply that they cannot see anything or find a way. The translation puts the same thing into four words: unable to see.

The phrase works as a hinge for the verses around it. Verse 16 said the deal left them unguided. This verse ends with them unable to see. The next verse says they are deaf, dumb and blind, and will not return. The sūrah is walking a reader down a single road, and each verse adds one more description of a person who has stopped moving.

---
## Verse 2:18

> They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb, and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺

**THREE FACULTIES, THREE DOORS SHUT**

The verse names three faculties and takes them away in one breath: ***“They are ˹wilfully˺ deaf, dumb”*** Al-Jalālayn gives the plain reading of the pair. They are deaf to the truth, so they cannot hear it and accept it. They are dumb as regards goodness, unable to speak about it. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same in one line: they cannot hear the truth and accept it, and they cannot speak about it either.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the words as a description of behaviour rather than of bodies: they pretend to be deaf, they pretend to be dumb.
