That reading fits the sūrah, because these are men with working ears and working tongues. They heard the Qur'an recited in the mosque and they talked about it in the market. What they refused was the hearing that leads to acceptance and the speech that carries the truth onward.

Al-Alūsī and the author of al-Baḥr al-Muḥīṭ pause over the words themselves, because the sūrah's Arabic is being read aloud every day and the sound matters. Deafness in the language is a blockage in the ear, a blockage that keeps sound from arriving, and the root carries the sense of something sealed shut — a sealed water bottle is described with the same word. Dumbness is a damage in the tongue that stops speech. The plural form used here is the heavy plural, the one Arabic reserves for a quality that has taken a person over, so the description is not of a moment of silence but of a settled state.

Al-Bayḍawī adds a note on how to take the whole sentence. The words are applied to these men by way of comparison rather than transfer, because they blocked their ears from listening to the truth, refused to let their tongues speak it and would not use their eyes on the signs in front of them. A person who disables all three instruments should not be surprised when he is described by their failure. Qushayrī puts it in the language of the inner life: deaf to the call of the Real with the ears of their hearts, dumb in the conversation with Him that belongs to the deepest self, blind to the signs of His ordering of events.

Think of a man who is handed earplugs, a blindfold and a pad of tape at the door of a briefing that decides his future, and who puts them on himself, one by one, while the room waits. Nobody in that room can help him afterwards, and the verse is written in the language of his own choice.

**WHY THEY WILL NOT TURN BACK**

and adds the third along with the verdict: ***“and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺”***. The third faculty closes the set. Al-Jalālayn says they are blind to the path of guidance, so they cannot see it, and the sentence that follows explains the consequence: they will not come back from their error. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the last words the same way: they do not return from their disbelief and their straying. tafsir_initial notes that the commentators name the realities these men have lost — the good, the truth and guidance (al-Ṭabarī) — and that the return spoken of here is the return to the guidance the sūrah mentioned a few verses earlier.

The Qur'an returns to this description in many places, and tafsir_initial gathers a few of them: (2:171 — **“the example of the disbelievers is like a shepherd who calls out to ˹sheep˺ that hear nothing but a shout and a cry”**), (17:97 — **“We will gather them on the Day of Judgment prone, blind, dumb, and deaf”**), and the verses that speak of hearts that do not understand ((6:25; 6:39; 10:42-43)). The same three failures appear at the resurrection, which is the sūrah's way of saying that a person's inner condition in this life is the shape he will be raised in.

Kashānī connects the blindness to a verse that describes a wall rather than an illness: (36:9 — **“We have put a barrier before them and a barrier behind them, and have covered them up so they cannot see”**). The image of a man shut inside a corridor explains why the verse ends where it does. This is not a man who cannot find the road. He is standing on it with his back turned.

The two verses of comparison also end here, and the reader should notice the sequence they complete. The first picture left the men unable to see. This verse says they are deaf, dumb and blind, and that they will not return. The next picture begins with rain, thunder and lightning, and shows men who can still hear enough to be frightened. The Book has moved from a man in total darkness to a man who is still being shaken.

---
## Verse 2:19

> Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder, and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap for fear of death. And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺

**THE STORM THEY WERE CAUGHT IN**

The second picture arrives with a storm in it: ***“Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder”*** Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the structure of the passage. The sūrah has given two comparisons for the hypocrites, and the word or here opens the second one. The first compared them to a man who lit a fire and lost the light. The second compares them to travellers caught in a storm. The change of picture matches a change of people. The fire describes the man whose rejection has gone deep and who only carries the name of a believer for what it brings him. The storm describes the man who sees the truth, wants it at some moments and cannot bring himself to pay its price, so he moves a step forward and stops again.

Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the reading of the details that most of the commentators share. The rain from the sky stands for the Qur'an coming down. The thunder stands for its warnings. The lightning stands for the flashes of truth that appear to them from time to time. Ibn ʿAbbās works the same three items through the verse and adds the texture: what came down from God contains darkness in the reports of trials and turmoil, thunder in the rebukes that put fear into a listener, and lightning in the explanations, the instruction and the promises. A single Book carries all three at once, which is why a person can be drawn to it and frightened of it in the same hour.

tafsir_initial records a small point of language that a modern reader may find surprising. The Arabic word for thunder was ordinary vocabulary among the Arabs before Islam, and the Qur'an uses it in that ordinary sense in another sūrah: (13:13 — **“the thunder glorifies Him with praise, and so do the angels out of awe of Him”**). Some commentators transmitted a report traced to Ibn ʿAbbās describing thunder as the name of an angel placed over the clouds, and other scholars treated that report as an addition the text does not need, since the word was already known to every Arab ear. The lesson is worth keeping: a sound word does not need a strange story to give it weight.

**FINGERS IN THE EARS**

and with the terror the storm brings: ***“and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap”*** ***“for fear of death.”*** The scene is deliberately physical, and the commentators keep it that way. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the men as plugging their ears because of the crash of thunder, out of fear of death. Al-Jalālayn adds that they are cautious of dying. Nobody in that picture is being accused of stupidity about the weather. They are being shown as men who will do anything to avoid hearing the thing that carries a threat, even while they walk through the country the threat is about.

The commentators then map the gesture onto the behaviour of the hypocrites and the Jews of Madinah, who stopped their ears when the Qur'an was recited with its promises and its warnings. Tazkirul Qur'an draws out the reason in a way that speaks to any generation. Fear is another thing that keeps a person from acknowledging the truth. Rain is a blessing, but it arrives with thunder and lightning, and a faint-hearted man runs from the blessing because of the company it keeps. Accepting God's word does the same thing to a person's life. It puts his position at risk for a while. He may clash with the people around him and with the way everyone else is doing things. So he stops where he is, or he moves one hesitant step, and the caution does not save him from anything at all. Those who do not answer the call with an open heart lose the blessing they were afraid to reach for.

Kashānī describes the inner mechanics of the same gesture. The man keeps away from listening because the message would cut him off from the pleasures he is living for, and from where he stands, giving those up feels the same as dying. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the picture in view for the reader: the covering of the ears answers to turning away from the truth and refusing to act on it.

Think of a town under a flood warning, with the siren going in the square and every door still shut. The people inside have not been misled about the water. They have simply decided that they would rather not hear about it.

**THE ONE WHO ENCOMPASSES THEM**

The verse then states the fact the men have forgotten: ***“And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺”***. Al-Mukhtaṣar explains the closing phrase: God surrounds the disbelievers and has complete power over them, and there is no way for them to escape Him. The word for encompassing is the word for something being closed in on all sides. It is the answer to the ears and the fingers and the turning away. A man can refuse to hear a warning. He cannot walk out of the country the warning is about.

That closing line also holds the door open for the rest of the sūrah. The men in the storm are described with the word disbelievers in this verse, though the passage began with hypocrites, and the commentators are comfortable with the overlap. Hypocrisy of this kind is disbelief wearing a name. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the same phrase ends the verse that comes after this one, where God says He could have taken away their hearing and their sight, and the repetition ties the two verses together.

The verse also carries a small grammatical detail that al-Qurṭubī discusses at length, because it shows how the Qur'an handles repeated scenes. The little word for when that opens the description points to a future or repeated event, so the picture is not one storm on one afternoon. It is the standing condition of a certain kind of person, and the Book tells the reader what to expect when he meets him in any century.

---
## Verse 2:20

> It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight. Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light, but when darkness covers them, they stand still. Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**THE LIGHT THAT ALMOST TOOK THEIR SIGHT**

The picture sharpens: ***“It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight.”*** Al-Qurṭubī opens his discussion of this verse with the verb the translation renders as about to. In Arabic it means a thing is close to happening, so close that it has nearly begun, and the choice of word keeps the flash in the air. He notes the same construction in another sūrah: (24:43 — **“the flash of its lightning almost takes away the eyesight”**), where the same near-miss is described. The men are not being struck blind. They are being shown how close they are standing to a light they cannot handle.

Al-Jalālayn reads the phrase as the light taking their sight away swiftly, if it were given the chance. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the brightness almost blinds them. Ibn Kathīr places the whole scene inside the inner life: the sūrah is describing hypocrites who recognise the truth in some moments and doubt it in others, so that when the doubt and confusion come their hearts are like the rainstorm. The lightning is the moment of clarity, and the near-blindness is what clarity does to a man who has spent his life looking away.

**WALKING BY WHATEVER LIGHT ARRIVES**

The men take what the storm gives them: ***“Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light,”*** Al-Jalālayn gives the movement plainly: whenever the flash gives them light, they walk in it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the same clause as the moment the hypocrites declared themselves believers and travelled along with the community for as long as the outward faith was accepted. That is the rhythm of the whole comparison. The light comes, the men move, and the moving is real while it lasts.

Al-Jalālayn then adds the observation that turns the weather into a portrait of the mind. The lightning stands for the arguments of the Qur'an as they strike a heart, and the men's response is mixed. They acknowledge the truths they happen to like and they pull back from the ones they dislike. That is a person choosing from a menu rather than submitting to a message. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the movement on the ground: whenever the lightning flashes and lights things up, they go forward.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān describes the same people from the outside: they move a step or two when the flash comes, and when the sky goes dark again they stop where they are. A man who walks only in the light of someone else's flash is not on a road of his own choosing. He is being carried forward in short bursts by a thing he cannot control, and the verse leaves him exactly there.

**STANDING STILL WHEN THE LIGHT GOES**

and when it stops they stop: ***“but when darkness covers them, they stand still.”*** ***“Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing”*** Al-Jalālayn reads the clause as men who stop and stand where they are. The Arabic describes a person rooted to the spot, and the sūrah uses the same verb elsewhere for the moment a thing simply comes to rest. Al-Mukhtaṣar says they remain in the darkness, unable to move.

The commentators apply this half to the moments after the flash passes: doubt returns, the community's arguments stop pressing on them, and the men do nothing at all. They do not go back, and they do not go on. Ibn Kathīr treats this hesitation as the defining mark of the second group among the hypocrites, the ones who know the truth and cannot bring themselves to live by it. Tazkirul Qur'an describes them in the same terms: fear of losing what they have, and fear of clashing with what everyone else does, keeps them from answering God's call with an open heart.

Notice how carefully the sūrah has drawn the difference between the two pictures it gives in these verses. The man in the fire parable loses the light altogether and stands in the dark. The travellers in the storm lose it and get it back, over and over, until the storm ends. One has lost the road. The other is being worn down by it.

**THE POWER THAT HOLDS THE WHOLE SCENE**

***“and sight.”*** The verse closes on the power behind the whole scene: ***“Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the clause as a statement of what God could have done with the outer faculties in the same way He had already done with the inner ones. They hear the thunder and see the lightning with working organs, and the verse reminds them that the organs are on loan. Al-Mukhtaṣar adds the reason the phrase is there at all: God has power over all things, and if He took their hearing and sight away, He would not be handing them back, because they keep turning away from the truth.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the words in the setting of the whole comparison. The thunder stands for the rebukes in the Qur'an and the lightning for its instructions and promises, and if God so willed, He could put an end to both, just as He could put an end to the hearing and the sight of the hypocrites and the Jews who kept their distance from the message. The verse takes the two faculties the men were using to keep the truth at arm's length — the ear they blocked and the eye they turned — and reminds them who made both.

There is comfort in the sentence as well, and the commentators do not miss it. The faculties are still in place. The thunder still comes, and the flashes still come, and the men in the storm are still being addressed. Kashānī describes the state as one of a man whose inner eye is clouded by sleep, so that the light of guidance reaches him and lifts him a little, and then the weight comes back. The verse says that the lifting and the weight are both in the hands of the One who could end the story whenever He chose.

Think of a driver on a road where the streetlights keep failing. When a lamp catches, he moves. When the road goes black, he stops and waits in the middle of the lane. He is still on the road, and he is still the reason he is not further along it.

**WHY THE PARABLE ENDS ON POWER**

Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the closing sentence as the ground of everything the passage has said: God has absolute power over all things. The men who cannot be moved by argument, the light that comes and goes, the hearing and the sight that could be withdrawn in a moment — all of it sits under one authority. The sūrah closes its account of the hypocrites with the widest possible statement about the One who is dealing with them.

The phrase also ends the introduction, and a reader can see the shape it leaves behind. Verses one to five presented the believers, and ended with the successful. Verses six and seven presented the open rejecters, and ended with a tremendous punishment. Verses eight to twenty presented the men with two faces, and end with power over everything. The next verse begins with a call addressed to all people, and the Book starts again from the beginning, speaking now to whoever is listening.

Ibn Kathīr notes that the two comparisons used in these last verses are the ones the sūrah will keep returning to as the chapter goes on. Hypocrisy of this kind does not end with one conversation in one city. It is a habit of mind that shows up whenever a person wants the benefits of faith without the surrender faith asks for. The sūrah names the condition on its opening page, and then spends its length teaching the reader what to do with it.

---
## Verse 2:21

> O humanity! Worship your Lord, Who created you and those before you, so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺

**THE CALL THAT OPENS THE ADDRESS TO EVERYONE**

The verse opens with a call to the whole human family: ***“O humanity!”*** ***“Worship your Lord,”*** The sūrah has finished sorting people into three groups, and it now speaks to everyone at once. Al-Saʿdī calls this a general command given to all people, and he explains what the word for worship covers here. It means doing what God has ordered, staying away from what He has forbidden and believing what He has reported. He points to the verse that states the purpose behind the whole arrangement: (51:56 — **“I did not create jinn and humans except to worship Me”**).

Al-Jalālayn reads the command in its first setting, among the people of Makkah, as a call to declare God one. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the same reading, and adds that some of the commentators took the address to be aimed at the Jews of Madinah. Al-Qurṭubī explains why the address is worded this way. He records the rule that ʿAlqamah and Mujāhid repeated, that every passage opening with O humanity belongs to Makkah and every passage opening with O believers belongs to Madinah, and then he notes its flaw himself. This sūrah is a Madinan sūrah and it contains this Makkah-style address. So the wording tracks the audience and not the calendar. When the call goes out to everyone, it says everyone; when it turns to the Muslims in particular, it says believers.

ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr gave al-Qurṭubī a rule that fits this sūrah well: legal limits and obligations came down in Madinah, while the stories of earlier nations and their punishments belong to Makkah. A voice that names no group and asks no group to stand apart is a voice for the whole street, and the sūrah uses it here before it starts giving law.

**THE REASON THE COMMAND IS REASONABLE**

The command is given with its reason attached: ***“Who created you and those before you”***, The verse gives the reason for the command, and the reason is older than the listener. Al-Jalālayn says: He made you when you were nothing, and made those who came before you. Ibn Kathīr describes the same act as God bringing His servants to life after they did not exist, and he treats the whole sūrah's teaching on God's oneness in worship as built on that one fact.

Al-Saʿdī presses the word Lord itself. God is the One who raised you and kept raising you with one kind of favour after another. A creature that arrived from nothing, and that owes its food, its air and its years to somebody else, has a very short road to the conclusion of the verse. The address also puts the listener in a line of people rather than on a stage alone. Everyone he can name from history was made the same way, by the same hand, and the verse says so in the plainest terms.

Al-Saʿdī's argument runs from creation to gratitude and then from gratitude to worship. Nobody thanks a benefactor by handing the credit to a stranger. The verse has already given the credit to God before it asks for anything, and what it asks for afterwards is the response that fits the account.

**WHAT WORSHIP IS MEANT TO PRODUCE**

and the sentence ends with the purpose it serves: ***“so that you may become mindful ˹of Him˺”***. The last phrase names the fruit of worship. Al-Jalālayn reads it as fear of God's punishment, and he notes that the Arabic form used for so that is a form of hoping, which when spoken by God carries the force of a promise rather than a wish. Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the practical sense: worship so that you may save yourselves from suffering by following God's instructions and keeping away from what He has forbidden. Ibn ʿAbbās says the same: obey Him so that you ward off His wrath and His punishment.

tafsir_initial sets out the difficulty the scholars discussed here, because the phrase can puzzle a careful reader. The word pictures a hope, and hope belongs to creatures who do not know how things will end. If God is the speaker, then either the hope must be read as a settled promise, as al-Zamakhsharī held, or the hope belongs to the person being addressed rather than to God. Either way the meaning lands in the same place. The worshipper hopes, and God guarantees the thing hoped for.

The word for mindfulness is worth taking slowly, because the whole sūrah will keep returning to it. It is not a mood and it is not a feeling. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes it as a way of protecting yourself, which is what a person does when he knows that something serious is at stake. Think of a man who learns to drive carefully. He is not afraid of the road; he is awake on it. That is the state this verse asks a person to build out of worship, one day at a time.

---
## Verse 2:22

> ˹He is the One˺ Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy; and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow as a provision for you. So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺

**THE SENTENCE THAT CARRIES ON FROM THE VERSE BEFORE**

The verse continues the description of the One being worshipped: ***“˹He is the One˺”*** Ibn Kathīr explains why the sentence takes this shape. After calling people to worship, the sūrah describes the One being worshipped, and it does so by listing what He has already done. Ibn Kathīr adds that God has surrounded His servants with blessings both hidden and apparent, and this verse takes the visible ones and lays them out where a farmer, a sailor or a shepherd would recognise them immediately.

Al-Saʿdī keeps the same two categories in view. The blessings a person can count and the blessings he never notices both come from the same source, and the verse names the largest ones in the order of their size: the ground under his feet, the roof over his head, the rain, and then the food on his table.

**A DWELLING AND A CANOPY**

Al-Jalālayn describes the earth here as a couch or a carpet, and he adds a detail that shows how carefully the Arabic was chosen: the ground is neither so hard that a person cannot stand firm on it, nor so soft that he cannot walk. It was measured for the body that lives there. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the earth was spread out in front of us like a carpet.

Al-Saʿdī unpacks what settling on the ground involves. You rest on it. You build on it, farm it, plough it and travel across it from one place to another. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verb translated as made carries the sense of turning one thing into another, so the verse describes a planet being fitted out for habitation rather than simply existing.

Think of a guest room prepared before a visitor arrives: the bed level, the floor swept, the light coming in through a window someone opened on purpose. The verse reads the planet in that spirit, and it says who prepared the room.

**RAIN, FRUIT, AND WHAT THEY PROVE**

***“Who has made the earth a place of settlement”*** ***“for you and the sky a canopy”***, Ibn Kathīr reads the word translated as canopy as a ceiling over the head. He points to another sūrah where the same idea is stated with a different image: (21:32 — **“And We have made the sky a well-protected canopy, still they turn away from its signs”**). Both verses describe a structure that holds, and both verses notice that the people living under it rarely look up at it.

Al-Saʿdī lists what the covering contains: the sun that fixes the day, the moon that marks the months, the stars that guide travellers, and the atmosphere that holds what the earth needs. He notes that the Arabic word for sky simply means everything above a person, which is why the commentators say that in this verse it refers to the clouds. The verse has no interest in the astronomy of a particular century. It points at the roof and asks the reader to notice that it has never needed repairs.

The asbāb al-nuzūl material attached to this passage reaches for poetry to make the same point. It describes the seven domes placed upon the wind without a pillar or a joint, and the seven dust-coloured layers resting on the water, then quotes the two verses that go with each: (15:16 — **“We have placed constellations in the sky and adorned it for all to see”**), and (51:48 — **“We have spread out the earth—how well We spread it!”**). The writer of that passage calls the reader to look at the pair of them, the black and the white, the dome and the ground, and to read them as one arrangement rather than two accidents.

**THE CONCLUSION THE EVIDENCE DEMANDS**

***“and sends down rain from the sky, causing fruits to grow”*** ***“as a provision for you.”*** The gifts are listed, and then the demand is drawn from them: Al-Jalālayn keeps the verb active and the chain short: God sends water down from the sky and brings forth every kind of fruit with it. Ibn ʿAbbās says the rain causes fruits of different kinds to appear. Al-Saʿdī names the ones a reader in that region would see in the market: grain, dates, fruit, crops.

The asbāb al-nuzūl material joins this clause to the ones before it. The wonders of power and the marks of wisdom in the earth and the sky are put forward as evidence of a Lord and a Maker, and as witnesses to His oneness, His knowledge and His ability. Water is the middle link in a chain that no human craftsman can copy end to end. A person can carry a bucket to a field. He cannot put a cloud in the sky above it.

The order of the verse also deserves a look. The ground is named first because it is what the reader is standing on. The sky comes second because it is what he sees when he steps outside at night. The rain comes third because it is the thing he waits for in a dry country, and the fruit comes last because it is what he eats. The Book builds the sentence the way the day builds a person's attention.

**THE PROVISION THAT NAMES ITS GIVER**

***“So do not knowingly set up equals to Allah ˹in worship˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the provision as food for the people addressed and for every creature that shares the earth with them. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the produce as coming from God's grace rather than from anything the soil or the farmer supplied on its own.

The word for provision carries a second sense that the commentators often mention elsewhere, and it is worth holding here. What a person receives is measured, delivered at a time it was not necessarily expected, and owed to somebody other than his own skill. tafsir_initial notes that the fruit spoken of in this verse has been read in a wider sense as well, as food for the body and as something that feeds the inner life. A harvest that appears every year on the same schedule is easy to treat as a fact of nature rather than a gift. The verse refuses that reading by naming the Giver in the same sentence as the gift.

Picture a household where a meal appears on the table every evening, and the person who eats it has never once asked where it came from. The verse asks one question and then moves on: who has been doing the cooking?

**THE EQUALS THE BOOK FORBIDS**

Al-Jalālayn builds the argument into the command. Do not set up partners, he says, when you know that God is the Creator and that you create nothing, and that only the One who creates can be God. Ibn ʿAbbās records the same reasoning and adds a second reading of the closing word: some of the commentators said it means you know from your own scripture that God has no son, no equal and no peer. Al-Mukhtaṣar adds the reason the word knowingly is there. A person is not being asked to avoid a mistake he could not help. He is being asked to stop doing something he already knows the answer to.

tafsir_initial gives the report that shows how heavy this offence is in the Book's own scale. The Prophet ﷺ was asked what the greatest sin is, and he replied: to set up equals to God though He created you. Ibn Kathīr records the same exchange from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd and notes that it is reported in the Two Sahīhs, the collections of al-Bukhārī and Muslim. In the full report the questioner goes on to ask about the next greatest sin, and the Prophet ﷺ names the killing of a child for fear of sharing food, and then dishonesty with a neighbour's wife.

The verse also sets the standard by which the rest of the sūrah can be read. Everything God asks for later — prayer, charity, fasting, honesty in trade — rests on the sentence at the end of this verse. A building with one foundation and many rooms is easier to understand than a pile of rules with no floor under it, and the verse is the floor.

---
## Verse 2:23

> And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true

**THE CHALLENGE AND WHO IT IS AIMED AT**

The challenge is put in the verse's own words: ***“And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant”***, Al-Ṭabarī opens his commentary on this verse by naming the people being addressed: the idolaters of the Arab tribes, the hypocrites among them, and the disbelievers of the two earlier communities, the groups whose stories opened this sūrah. The verse is God's case for His Prophet, set out in a form anyone could test. Al-Qurṭubī reports the words the doubters used. They listened to the Qur'an and said this does not look like the speech of God, and we are in doubt about it.

Al-Jalālayn identifies the servant as Muḥammad ﷺ and the revelation as the Qur'an. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the tone of the sentence: the verse speaks to anyone who carries doubt about the Book sent down to God's servant. The wording puts the doubt on the table without insult and then hands the doubter a way out of it.

Al-Saʿdī draws attention to the fairness of the arrangement, and this is a point worth pausing over. The Prophet ﷺ was a man from their own town, known to them from childhood, and he neither read nor wrote. His accusers knew all of that. If the Book were his own invention, as they claimed, then the men who produced the best Arabic of their generation had every advantage over him. The verse invites them to use it.

Al-Qurṭubī explains the word servant here as the highest title a creature can carry. It comes from a root that means to submit and to humble oneself, and the verse gives the Prophet ﷺ that name at the very moment it defends him. The one being defended is the one who claimed nothing for himself.

**WHAT IS BEING ASKED FOR**

***“then produce a sûrah like it”*** Al-Jalālayn explains what is being asked. A sūrah is a passage with a beginning and an ending, and in this context it means at least three verses. The demand is to produce a passage like the Qur'an in its eloquence, in its arrangement, and in what it tells of things unseen. He adds the detail that makes the challenge sharp: the men being addressed were fluent speakers of Arabic too, so nobody was being asked to do something outside his language.

tafsir_initial sets out the term that the scholars later coined for this subject. The Qur'an's power to stop an opponent in his tracks is studied under the name of iʿjāz, and it rests on its beauty, its clarity, its eloquence and its layers of meaning. The Book extends the same challenge in several places: (10:38 — **“Produce one sûrah like it then, and seek help from whoever you can—other than Allah”**), (11:13 — **“Produce ten fabricated sûrahs like it”**), and (17:88 — **“If ˹all˺ humans and jinn were to come together to produce the equivalent of this Quran, they could not produce its equal”**). Once the challenge names ten sūrahs, once one, and once the whole Book, and the Book also records that nobody met it. tafsir_initial notes the background that gives the challenge its weight: the Prophet ﷺ was known among his people as a man who did not read (see 7:157).

Al-Saʿdī makes the same point as a matter of fair play. The men claimed the Book was invented. If it was invented, they could invent something like it. They had the language, the poets, the time and the motive, and no answer has ever been produced. A challenge that stands unanswered for fourteen centuries is itself part of the evidence.

**THE HELPERS THEY WERE TOLD TO CALL**

***“and call your helpers other than Allah”*** Ibn ʿAbbās reads the word for helpers as the gods they worshipped, and he records a second reading naming their leaders instead. Al-Jalālayn comments that the instruction was to call on every other god they had, so that it would become obvious whether they were telling the truth when they said the Prophet ﷺ had made the Book up himself.

Al-Saʿdī points out how generous the invitation is. It is not addressed to one poet or one clan. Every descendant of a tribe, every partner, every power they trusted could be brought in to help, and the verse says so. Nobody is excluded from the contest except the One whose Book is being measured.

Witnesses in the Arabic here carries the sense of people who could testify that a piece of writing matched the Book in quality. The verse is asking for a panel of judges and a set of assistants at the same time, and it lets the doubters choose both.

**THE CONDITION THAT CLOSES THE OFFER**

***“if what you say is true”***. The sentence closes the offer with its own condition. Al-Mukhtaṣar reads it as a challenge to the claim: bring a sūrah and call your helpers, if you are truthful in what you say. Al-Jalālayn reads it the same way and adds the contrast that runs through the passage: the word for truthfulness is set here beside the doubt that opened the verse.

Al-Ṭabarī notes the location of this verse in the flow of the sūrah. The passage before it argued for God's oneness from the sky, the earth and the harvest. This passage argues for the Prophet's truthfulness from the Book itself. The two arguments follow the same pattern: God does something no one else can do, and the conclusion is that nobody else can be given His place, whether that place is Creator or Speaker.

The verse's last three words also function as a hinge for what follows, because the next verse takes the offer away. A person who has been asked to prove his case, and who cannot, is left with the Fire in front of him. That is a hard turn, and the sūrah makes no apology for it. It gives the doubter a fair test, an open invitation to help and then a plain warning about what the delay costs.

---
## Verse 2:24

> But if you are unable to do so—and you will never be able to do so—then fear the Fire fuelled with people and stones, which is prepared for the disbelievers

**THE SENTENCE THEY CANNOT ESCAPE**

The verse ends the argument with the words they cannot answer: ***“But if you are unable to do”*** ***“so and you will never be able to do”***, Al-Ṭabarī explains the first half of the sentence as a matter already settled in the listeners' own experience. If they did not bring a sūrah, with all their helpers standing behind them, then their own test has made the matter clear to them and to every creature: they could not do it, and the Book did not come from a human hand. He records the report of Qatādah on the words you will never be able to do: you are not able to do it and you will never have the strength for it.

Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report carried through ʿIkrimah and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, reads the whole clause as a statement that the truth has been made plain to them. tafsir_initial points out the grammatical shape that carries this certainty. A human being says if I can and means maybe. When the Book says if you do not, and follows it with you never will, there was never a possibility in play. The sentence is built like a door being closed.

Al-Jalālayn describes the middle phrase as a parenthetical remark dropped into the sentence, and its function is exactly what he says. Everything the challenge offered is over before the warning arrives. The men are not being asked to try harder. They are being told what to do now that the question has been answered.

**THE FUEL THAT DESCRIBES THE FIRE**

***“so then fear the Fire fuelled with people”*** ***“and stones, which is prepared”*** Al-Jalālayn explains what the fuel tells a reader about the fire. Wood is what fires burn in this world, and a fire that burns people and stones is of another order, since its heat must consume what cannot burn. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the stones as sulphur. Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the sense that ties the picture back to the previous verses: the fire burns the people who earned the punishment, and it burns the idols they used to worship instead of God, so that both the worshippers and the things they worshipped are dealt with.

The Book states the same arrangement in another place, and Ibn Kathīr brings it in: (21:98 — **“Certainly you ˹disbelievers˺ and whatever you worship instead of Allah will be the fuel of Hell”**), and (66:6 — **“Protect yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones”**). The stones a person hoped would speak for him end up in the same furnace as the hands that carried them there. Al-Mukhtaṣar notes that this verse is the one that uses the same image at the end of the sūrah's opening warning.

tahsir_initial records the second reading of the stones, that they are the idols themselves, which the polytheists of that region made from stone. Either way the picture is the same and the point is sharpened. The things a person trusts in place of God become part of what is thrown in after him.

**WHO THE FIRE WAS PREPARED FOR**

***“for the disbelievers”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the last phrase as a statement that the fire has been made ready, in advance, for the people who reject. He notes the alternative grammatical reading as well: the phrase can be treated as a description attached to the fire rather than a separate sentence. Either way the fire is not being improvised at the moment of reckoning. It is waiting, and the warning in the verse arrives long before the appointment.

Ibn Kathīr closes the section by pointing to the argument the passage has made from beginning to end. The sūrah proved God's oneness from what He has made, and then proved the Prophet's truthfulness from what he brought. Every line of evidence has one conclusion, and the verse states the price of ignoring it without softening the language. The offer to produce a sūrah still stands, but nobody has taken it, and the warning that follows is the next thing on the page.

Think of a contest announced with an open door and a deadline. The rival has been told to bring his best work and any help he wants. When the deadline passes and the door is shut, the same announcement turns into a notice about what happens to the loser. This verse is that notice, and it is written for people who still have time to read it.

---
## Verse 2:25

> Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow. Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,” for they will be served fruit that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺. They will have pure spouses, and they will be there forever

**GOOD NEWS AND THE PEOPLE IT BELONGS TO**

The verse opens with good news: ***“Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those”*** ***“who believe and do good”*** Al-Qurṭubī explains the word behind the translation. Good news in Arabic is any true report that changes the expression on a person's face, because the skin responds before the mind has finished hearing it. The word is used for good news and for bad, but almost always for good, and where the Qur'an uses it for punishment the text says so plainly.

Ibn Kathīr points out the order of the sūrah here. The verses before this one closed with a fire prepared for the rejecters, and this verse opens with the reward of the friends of God. He notes that the Qur'an is called Mathānī, the paired one, because it keeps setting mercy beside punishment so that a reader holds both in one hand. Al-Saʿdī says the same thing about the method: the Book pairs warning with encouragement so that a servant lives between hope and fear.

The condition in this verse is stated in two halves, and both matter. They believe with their hearts, and they do good with their hands. Al-Saʿdī adds the word good to the work because good works are the ones that put a person's affairs in order — his religion, his livelihood, this life and the next — and remove the disorder from them. Al-Baghawī records the explanation ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān gave for the phrase: it means work done sincerely, without showing off. Muʿādh ibn Jabal said that a good work carrying all four of its parts has knowledge, intention, patience and sincerity. Lose any one of the four and the work is not whole.

Kashānī describes the verse as a kindness shown to the friends of God, giving them hope of lasting generosity. He pictures a believer hearing the previous verse about the fire and then this verse about the Garden, and says the fear of the first and the relief of the second are both praised. A heart that is only frightened becomes hard. A heart that is only comfortable becomes careless.

**GARDENS THAT RUN WITH RIVERS**

***“that they will have Gardens under”*** ***“which rivers flow. Whenever provided with fruit, they will say, “This is what we were given before,””*** Al-Jalālayn explains the word rivers here the way the Arabic uses it: a river is the channel that water carves through a place, and the water in it is described as running. He notes that the phrase speaks of water flowing below the trees and the buildings rather than underground. tafsir_initial confirms this reading from al-Ṭabarī: the water flows lower than the vegetation of the Garden, which is the opposite of the way water sits in a desert, where it is almost never above ground and almost never moving.

Ibn ʿAbbās lists the rivers a reader will find in the descriptions of the Garden elsewhere in the Book: rivers of wine, of milk, of honey and of water. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the gardens as holding rivers that flow beneath the palaces and the trees. Al-Jalālayn notes that the place itself is called a garden because its trees cover it, and the word for covering is where the name comes from.

tafsir_initial makes a point about these pictures that a modern reader needs, because the images are physical and someone will ask whether they are only metaphors. The descriptions of the Garden use concrete terms drawn from what people already know, and they stand for joys far beyond what the words can carry. They are not less than what they say. They are more. The eye has seen nothing like what is being kept for the people described in this verse.

**THE FRUIT THAT TASTES LIKE HOME**

***“for they will be served fruit”*** ***“that looks similar ˹but tastes different˺. They will have pure spouses”*** Al-Jalālayn explains the sentence in two steps. The people of the Garden are given fruit, and it resembles fruit they have eaten before, so they say this is like what we were given earlier. Then the Book explains the resemblance: the fruit is served in a perfect likeness, alike in colour and in name, and different in taste. Ibn ʿAbbās says the same: it is given in resemblance as far as colour goes, and the taste is not the same.

Al-Mukhtaṣar adds the reason for the resemblance. The fruit carries the same name and the same shape so that the people recognise it and want it, and the taste is different so that the joy keeps arriving as a surprise. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the verse the same way and says the point is a kind of joy the believers never knew before, renewing itself at every moment. He also allows the other reading — that the fruits of the Garden are like one another in shape and always different in taste — and notes that both readings reach the same conclusion.

The word translated as before is worth a pause. tafsir_initial explains that it refers to this world rather than to an earlier moment in the Garden, so the comparison is between the food of the earth and the food of the Hereafter. The people of the Garden taste a fruit and are reminded of a taste they knew in a kitchen or an orchard long ago. That is the arrangement in one line: something familiar enough to be loved, and better than anything the memory can hold.

Think of a family dish cooked by the same hands every year at the same season. The plate looks the way it has always looked, and the first bite is never the same as last year's. The verse takes that small domestic experience and builds the Garden out of it.

**PURE COMPANIONS AND A STAY WITHOUT END**

***“and they will be there forever”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the word for pure as a description of what these spouses are free from: the things that make bodies on this earth difficult, and every kind of filth. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same in a modern sentence: they are paired with mates who have none of the unpleasant traits that people carry here. Al-Jalālayn adds the clear note that the purity includes what the women of this world are subject to.

Then the verse closes with time, or rather with the end of it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads forever as never dying and never being sent out. Al-Mukhtaṣar points at what makes the promise so large: the joys of this world are temporary, and every one of them is interrupted. The Garden is the one house where nothing on the schedule is a last time.

The verse has run through the whole list a person might ask about: a place that holds gardens, water that keeps moving, food that keeps surprising, company that keeps its sweetness, and time that does not run out. Al-Saʿdī notes that the pairing of warning and encouragement in this sūrah is not decoration. The readers being addressed were the first community that had to carry this message in a city that was watching them, and they needed to know what was waiting at the other end of the road.

---
## Verse 2:26

> Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito or what is even smaller. As for the believers, they know that it is the truth from their Lord. And as for the disbelievers, they argue, “What does Allah mean by such a parable?” Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray, and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious—

**THE OBJECTION THE VERSE ANSWERS**

The verse answers an objection the men had raised: ***“Surely Allah does not shy away from using the parable of a mosquito”*** The verse answers an objection, and the objection had a setting. Ibn Kathīr reports through al-Suddī from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions that when God gave the two parables of the hypocrites — the man who lit a fire and the travellers in the storm — the hypocrites said that God is far above using such examples. Al-Qurṭubī records the same report and adds a second setting: when the Qur'an mentioned the fly that the idols could not create even if they all gathered to it ((22:73)), and called the shelter of the idol-worshippers the web of a spider ((29:41 — **“the parable of those who take protectors other than Allah is that of a spider spinning a shelter”**)), the Jews said that God was too exalted to speak of such lowly creatures.

The sūrah answers both complaints with the same word. Al-Jalālayn explains what the Arabic means here: God is not prevented from striking a comparison by anything that would make a person ashamed. Al-Baghawī records the root of the word, which turns on shame and on being held back by it. The objection assumed that the size of a creature limits the dignity of its Maker. The verse says the opposite: a Maker is free to use anything He has made, and the smallness of the creature is not the smallness of its Designer.

Al-Saʿdī draws out the lesson and turns it toward the objector. Every comparison in the Book carries wisdom and makes the truth clearer, and God is not ashamed of the truth. He says that this clause is an answer to anyone who objected to parables drawn from lowly things, and that such an objection has no basis. What the objector was treating as an insult was God teaching His servants and showing them mercy, and the right response is to receive it with acceptance and thanks.

Ibn ʿAbbās adds a thought that closes the argument. How could God turn away from mentioning a creature that no created being could make, even if all of them gathered together for it? The comparison is not about the price of the animal. It is about who owns it.

**DOWN TO A GNAT, AND SMALLER**

***“or what is even smaller.”*** Al-Jalālayn explains the phrase as anything above the gnat, meaning anything larger, so that the objection never grows with the size of the creature. He compares it to a man who refuses one coin and then refuses a larger sum on the same grounds. tafsir_initial records the second reading that the commentators gave, since the word can also carry the sense of something further on in the same direction. On that reading the phrase names something more lowly than a gnat, and the verse becomes a statement about the range of God's comparisons rather than their size.

Al-Saʿdī states the principle that governs both readings. God strikes whatever comparison He chooses, and the wisdom is in the comparison and not in the creature used to make it. A mirror is not valued for what it is made of. It is valued for what it shows, and the answer to a bad reflection is not to smash the glass.

**WHAT FAITH DOES WITH A SMALL PARABLE**

***“As for the believers, they know”*** ***“that it is the truth from their Lord.”*** Al-Jalālayn reads the clause as a statement about what faith does with a hard text. The believers know that the comparison is the truth established from their Lord, and that is the end of the matter for them, whether or not every detail is clear. Al-Saʿdī explains the mechanics of that knowledge. They understand the parable and think about it. If they grasp what it contains in detail, their knowledge and their faith increase. If some part of it stays beyond them, they still know that it is the truth, and that what it carries is the truth, because they know their Lord did not strike it as a joke. He did it for a deep wisdom and an overflowing blessing.

That is a working rule for a reader of any age, and the sūrah states it here in the middle of an argument. The Book contains passages that open immediately and passages that stay closed for years. A believer's position on the closed ones is not blankness or denial. It is trust in the Speaker while the meaning is still arriving.

Kashānī puts the difference between the two reactions in a single image: the man who believes is the one who benefits from what he is shown, and the man who refuses is the one who argues with the mirror for showing him his face.

**WHAT DENIAL DOES WITH IT**

***“And as for the disbelievers, they argue, “What does Allah mean by such a parable?””*** The verse records the objection in their own words, and the words are worth reading slowly. They do not ask what the parable means. They ask what God meant by saying anything at all. Al-Jalālayn explains the grammar as a question of rejection rather than inquiry, the tone a man uses when he wants an answer and has already decided to refuse it. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the same reaction with its examples: the disbelievers make fun and ask each other why God would use lowly creatures such as gnats, flies and spiders.

Ibn ʿAbbās reports the same scene as a scene of mockery among the Jews of Madinah. Al-Saʿdī notes what happens inside a person who takes this road. The objector does not gain information and does not gain peace. He gets confusion, and his rejection grows into more rejection, since a heart that refuses one clear thing becomes readier to refuse the next. The verse names the argument, and then the next clause names the cost.

The Book keeps this objection on the record for a reason, and the reason is that it keeps being made. People in every century have decided in advance what God may and may not say, and then treated their own rule as reverence. The verse answers that habit in advance by showing what it sounds like from the outside: a creature small enough to need a gnat's blood for its own lessons, objecting to the size of the lesson.

**THE TEST THAT SEPARATES TWO RESPONSES**

***“Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray”*** Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the plain sense: these comparisons carry guidance, direction and a test. Many people are led astray through them because they refuse to think about them, and many are guided because they learn from them. Al-Saʿdī describes the test as a display of what is inside each listener. The same sentence reaches two hearts and produces opposite results, and the sentence did not change.

tafsir_initial explains the mechanism in the sūrah's own terms. The verse says that the message both guides and misleads, which is different from a message that is simply ignored. When a man is shown a clear sign and refuses it, his refusal itself shifts him further from the truth. This is the same pattern the sūrah described earlier when it said that God lets the sickness of the doubters increase, and it will appear again in the verses about the sealing of hearts. The sign is not neutral for the person who rejects it.

tahsir_initial adds the naming: those who are left to stray by such a test are people who are already in the habit of stepping over boundaries, and the word used for them at the end of the verse is the word for that habit. The Book does not say that a careless curiosity costs a person his guidance. It says that a settled refusal does.

Think of a mirror in a hallway. Every person who walks past it sees his own face, and the mirror decides nothing for anyone. The ones who stop and straighten a collar leave better than they came. The ones who blame the glass for what it shows leave with the same face, and a small grudge. That is the shape of the test this verse describes.

**WHO IS LEFT TO STRAY**

***“and guides many. And He leaves none to stray except the rebellious”***. The verse closes by naming who is affected. Al-Mukhtaṣar explains that God does not misguide anyone except the person who deserves it, the one who does not obey Him. Ibn ʿAbbās reports the identification that the commentators of the first generations gave, tied to the objections in Madinah. tafsir_initial notes the word used at the end of the verse, which names a person who has gone outside the boundary rather than a person who has simply made a mistake.

Al-Saʿdī describes the condition as a departure. The heart moves from the place it was made to stand in, where it obeyed the Most Merciful, and settles into obedience to something else. Once a person has made that move on purpose, a clear sign does not pull him back; it pushes him further, because the sign shows him precisely what he has chosen against.

The next verse describes these same people in detail, and it is worth noticing how the sūrah joins the two halves. The parable that offended them was a test, and the ones it caught were already in the habit the next verse describes: they break their agreements, cut the ties God told them to keep, and spread damage in the land. The objection to a gnat was never about a gnat.

---
## Verse 2:27

> those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed, break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption in the land. It is they who are the ˹true˺ losers

**THE OATH THEY UNDID AND THE TIES THEY SEVERED**

The description is carried over from the verse before, and the verse names the offence: ***“those who violate Allah’s covenant after it has been affirmed,”*** ***“break whatever ˹ties˺ Allah has ordered to be maintained”*** Al-Jalālayn explains the covenant as the agreement God took from people in the revealed Books, that they would believe in Muḥammad ﷺ, an agreement these men undid after it had been bound fast. Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the other setting the commentators used: the covenant of the day when every soul was asked, Am I not your Lord, and answered yes ((7:172 — **“And ˹remember˺ when your Lord brought forth from the loins of the children of Adam their descendants and had them testify regarding themselves. ˹Allah asked,˺ ‘Am I not your Lord?’ They replied, ‘Yes, we bear witness!’”**)), a covenant later confirmed by the prophets and the scriptures. Al-Qurṭubī records three views of which agreement is meant: the day of the covenant, the pledge taken from the prophets to believe in the final Messenger ((3:81)), and the pledge recorded in the Torah to make the Prophet known.

Al-Qurṭubī also explains the verb, and his note is worth keeping. To violate in this context means to undo what has been firmly built or firmly tied. The word is used for a wall pulled down, a rope unravelled, a knot undone. He adds that a man's word is a knot, and the whole verse is about hands working at a knot that should be left alone.

The second half of the clause names the ties. Ibn ʿAbbās mentions oaths and kinship, and the connection to the Prophet ﷺ. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān opens the list wide: what God has ordered to be joined includes the bond between God and His servant, the bond between a man and his parents and relatives, between him and his neighbours and friends, and between one Muslim and another. He adds the sentence that makes the passage practical. This religion means fulfilling one's obligations in all of these relationships, and a shortfall in any of them produces disorder among people, so that the person who breaks the ties ends up harming others and himself.

**THE HARM THEY SPREAD AROUND THEM**

***“and spread corruption in the land. It is they”*** Ibn ʿAbbās reads the corruption here as the work of keeping people away from the religion and from the Qur'an. This is one of the two definitions of the word the sūrah has already given in its account of the hypocrites, and it fits the men being described. A person who tears up a road does visible damage. A person who stands at the road and waves travellers away does something harder to prove and just as destructive.

The demonstrative in the middle of the sentence is the sūrah's way of pointing at the group and stopping the reader in front of them. The verse has listed three actions — breaking the agreement, cutting the ties, damaging the land — and then it turns and says it is they, as if to make sure the reader has connected the acts to the people doing them. The Book has used the same device several times in the opening passage: they are the ones who trade guidance for misguidance, they are the ones who are the corrupters, and here they are the ones who are the losers.

Al-Saʿdī notes that the three offences run in a sequence, from the private agreement to the public damage. A man's inner loyalty loosens first. Then the ties around him start coming apart. Then the harm reaches the street.

**WHO THE LOSERS REALLY ARE**

***“who are the ˹true˺ losers”***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the loss as a double one: these are the people who lose this world and the Hereafter alike. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same and adds that the loss is already taking place in the present. Al-Jalālayn describes the end of the account: they finish in a fire that does not end.

The sūrah has used this word for loss once before, when it described the men who traded guidance for misguidance. The Book's idea of a bad deal returns at the end of the warning, and it returns at the end of a passage about agreements. A man who breaks a promise may believe he has saved himself something by doing it — his time, his money, his position. The verse takes that calculation and turns it over, and shows that what he saved was nothing beside what he paid.

Think of a man who pulls down a bridge to stop somebody else crossing it and then discovers that the road to his own market ran over the same bridge. That is the picture the two halves of this verse make together, and the sūrah's word for it is one word: losers.

---
## Verse 2:28

> How can you deny Allah? You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life, and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**THE QUESTION THE ARGUMENT RESTS ON**

The question is put to the deniers directly: ***“How can you deny Allah?”*** The sūrah asks a question, and the commentators describe what kind of question it is. Al-Jalālayn says the opening carries either astonishment at the way people persist in rejection while the evidence stands in front of them, or a plain rebuke. Al-Mukhtaṣar puts the same sentence in modern words: how strange is the state of a person who denies the oneness of God while he can see the signs of His power inside his own body.

Ibn ʿAbbās takes the sentence as a question aimed at the people of Makkah who heard it first. Ibn Kathīr treats it as proof that God exists and that He is the Maker and the Sustainer with full authority over His servants. He brings in the verse that closes every escape hatch at once: (52:35 — **“Or were they created by nothing, or were they the creators ˹of themselves˺?”**). A person who arrived from nothing, and who cannot say how, is in no position to write the question off.

The Qur'an's method in this sentence deserves a look, because it is a model for anyone who argues about faith. The verse does not begin with a demand. It begins with a piece of the listener's own biography, and it asks him to explain it.

**LIFE GIVEN, LIFE TAKEN, LIFE RETURNED**

***“You were lifeless and He gave you life”***, Al-Jalālayn reads the first state as the person before birth, described in the language of the time as a drop within the loins. Ibn ʿAbbās says the same: they were lifeless drops in the loins of their fathers, and God gave them life in the wombs of their mothers. Then life continued in this world with the breath that was put into them. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the first state simply as being nothing, and the second as being brought to life.

Al-Jalālayn records the two readings of first state that the scholars worked with. The Book calls it dead either because a person existed as part of the material of his parents, or because he was a thing not yet remembered, and the Arabs called a thing dead when every trace of it had been erased and no one mentioned it. tafsir_initial cites the verse that carries the second sense: (76:1 — **“Was there not a period of time when the human being was nothing to be mentioned?”**). Whichever reading a person takes, the point of the clause is the same. There was a time when he was not, and the change to being came from outside him.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the application. The verses before this one have given arguments for God's oneness and for the Prophet's truthfulness from the world outside, and this verse appeals to the listener's own private history, which he knows better than anyone. He has no memory of arranging his own arrival. The Book asks him to notice that, in the hope that gratitude will do what argument alone has not.

**DEATH AND WHAT FOLLOWS IT**

***“then He will cause you to die”*** Al-Jalālayn places the third stage when a person's term is complete. Ibn ʿAbbās says God will cause death when the lifespan ends. The verse does not soften the point, and the commentators do not either. The same hand that opened the door closes it.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that these stages are given to a reader as a single sequence, and that the sequence is the argument. Each stage is a favour or a proof, and the collection of them belongs to one Giver. The man who takes his arrival for granted, his life for granted and his death as a fact of nature has answered the verse's question in a way he cannot defend. Somebody set the term, and the Book says who.

Al-Saʿdī keeps the accounting in view even in this clause. The One who gave the life has the right to name the day it ends, and there is no injustice in a loan being called in.

**THE LIFE THEY NEVER COUNTED ON**

***“and again bring you to life”***, Al-Jalālayn says the fourth stage is the resurrection on the day of standing, and this is the point the verse was building towards. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the same. tafsir_initial shows how the Book counts the stages elsewhere: on that day the people will say, (40:11 — **“Our Lord! You made us lifeless twice, and gave us life twice. Now we confess our sins”**). Two deaths and two lives, and this verse puts all four in one sentence.

The sūrah will return to this argument later in the chapter with a homely image, and it is worth remembering that the whole case rests on the first act. A man who came from nothing once can be brought from nothing a second time, and the One who did it the first time has the record. Ibn Kathīr gathers the verses that state the reasoning in the same shape: (22:66 — **“He is the One Who gave you life, then will cause you to die, and then will bring you back to life”**).

Some of the mystics, Ibn al-ʿArabī among them as tafsir_initial records, read the first lifelessness as a state within God's knowledge rather than a state of nothingness. The verse holds that reading and the plainer one, because both of them say the same thing in different words: the person being addressed came later than the One addressing him.

**THE RETURN THAT ENDS THE ARGUMENT**

***“and then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the last stage as the return for the reckoning, where every person is paid for what he did. Ibn ʿAbbās says the same and adds the word account. Al-Mukhtaṣar closes the sentence in three movements: you will return to Him and be held responsible.

The verse has moved from the dust to the cradle, from the cradle to the grave, from the grave to the resurrection, and from the resurrection to a meeting. That is a whole human biography in half a line, and the sūrah uses it to answer a question about denial. A person who lives inside a story like this has a very small amount of room in which to be his own judge.

Think of a worker on a project who is told before he starts that the owner will arrive at the end of the season to review everything. He may do the work well or badly. What he cannot do is pretend that no such meeting is coming, because the schedule was handed to him on the first day. This verse is that schedule, and the next verse adds the workshop and the tools.

---
## Verse 2:29

> He is the One Who created everything in the earth for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens. And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

**THE ONE WHO MADE THE EARTH OF USE**

The answer to the question of the previous verse begins: ***“He is the One”*** Ibn Kathīr explains the placement of the verse. The sūrah has just spoken about the creation of the human being, and it now points to something else the same readers can see for themselves: the earth beneath them and the sky above them. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the same frame and calls the verse a continuation of the blessings named in the verses before it.

Al-Qurṭubī opens his commentary on this verse by explaining the word for created, which here means to bring something into existence after it did not exist. He records Ibn Kaysān's reading of the phrase for you: everything in the earth was made for your sake. He then reports the discussion among the scholars about why these facts are mentioned at all, and gives the answer he considers sound: the verse is evidence for God's oneness and a lesson for the people being addressed.

**EVERYTHING THE EARTH HOLDS**

***“Who created everything in the earth”*** Al-Mukhtaṣar lists what the phrase covers: the countless rivers, the trees and everything else people use and enjoy. Al-Jalālayn reads the plain sense: the earth and everything in it, given so that people may benefit from it and learn lessons from it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the word for everything as it stands, wide enough to include the animals and the plants and whatever else is under a person's feet.

Al-Saʿdī draws a ruling out of the verse that its wording supports. The verse is stated in the form of a favour, which shows that things are permitted and clean by default, since a gift named as a gift for us cannot be forbidden by the same sentence. He adds the exception the Book makes: what is harmful or impure is excluded from the permission, and the exclusion is itself part of the favour, because the Lord who gave the earth also kept His servants away from what would damage them.

That is a large door opened by four words, and it is the kind of thing this sūrah does often. A verse about the soil answers a question about law, because the way a gift is described tells you what the Giver intends for you.

**THE HEAVENS RAISED IN ORDER**

***“for you. Then He turned towards the heaven, forming it into seven heavens.”*** Ibn Kathīr reads turned towards as the act of turning attention to the creation of the sky, and he explains that the plural pronoun covering the heavens is why the verse says He formed them. Al-Qurṭubī and the linguists discussed the verb at length, and al-Saʿdī gathers the three senses it carries in the Book: when it stands alone it means that something reached its full maturity, as in the verse about Moses; when it comes with on it means rising high; and when it comes with to, as here, it means turning attention to a matter and taking a firm decision about it. The verse then describes a decided purpose and a finished structure, not a struggle that ended in success.

tafsir_initial collects the places where the seven heavens appear so that a reader has the whole map: (67:3 — **“˹He is the One˺ Who created seven heavens, one above the other”**), (41:12), (65:12), and (71:15) on their levels. The same note records that Mujāhid, one of the earliest commentators, described heavens above one another and earths above one another, so the picture in these verses is a layering rather than a single lid.

Kashānī reads the sky in the verse as something made for the human eye as well as for the human need: a place of provision and a thing of beauty to look at. He joins this verse to another that states the same favour in one movement: (45:13 — **“He ˹also˺ subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all by His grace”**). The gift runs from the ground under a person's sandals to the stars over his head, and the verse names both ends in one sentence.

**KNOWLEDGE THAT HAS NO GAPS**

***“And He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the closing phrase as knowledge of everything in its whole and in its detail, and then he draws the conclusion the verse is aiming at. The One able to create all of this must be able to bring people back after death, since raising a human being is smaller than building a sky. Ibn ʿAbbās reads it as knowledge of the creation of the heavens and the earth, and al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the statement general: God knows everything.

Ibn Kathīr notes that this attribute is not decoration at the end of a verse about creation. The argument of the passage has been that people should worship the Maker and not the made thing. A Maker who knows everything in detail is also able to keep an account of everything a person does with the gift. The verse closes the door on the idea that some corner of a life escapes notice because it is small or hidden.

The sūrah reaches the end of its opening section here, and a reader can see the shape of it. Verses 21 to 24 called all people to worship the One who made them, and answered the doubters with the challenge to produce a sūrah. Verses 25 to 29 described the reward of the believers, the test of the parable, the state of the men who break their agreements, and the evidence for God's power in the two creations. Verse 30 will begin the account of an older story, when God told the angels that He was placing a deputy on the earth.

Think of a workshop owner who buys a building, fits it out, stocks the shelves and writes the instruction manual before the first worker arrives on the first morning. The worker walks in and finds everything ready and everything accounted for, down to the smallest tool. The verses here read the earth and the sky the same way, and the last phrase is the owner's note: nothing in the workshop is out of his knowledge.

---
## Verse 2:30

> ˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.” They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood while we glorify Your praises and proclaim Your holiness?” Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.”

**THE DECISION ANNOUNCED TO THE ANGELS**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the turn the sūrah takes here. The verses before this one named the blessings a person can see: the earth, the sky, the rain, the fruit. This verse opens a story about blessings a person cannot put his hand on — knowledge, honour, and the trust that came with being made in this shape. The next ten verses tell the story of the father of the human race, and they do it as an illustration of the same argument about gratitude.

The verse tells the story of a conversation: ***“˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.””*** The question that follows is the objection: Ibn Kathīr reads the announcement as a favour shown to the children of Adam before any of them existed. God named them in the highest company before He created them, and the honour arrived before the person did. He explains the word translated as authority: it means people coming one generation after another, century after century. He points to another verse that states the same arrangement: (6:165 — **“He is the One Who has made you successors on the earth and raised some of you above others in rank”**).

tafsir_initial opens up the word that gives this verse its importance. A khalīfah is a deputy or a successor, and the term was later used for the head of the Muslim community as the successor of God's Messenger. In this verse and others like it, the word carries a wider sense: the human being as a whole is entrusted with the earth. Other verses use it for one people succeeding another ((7:69; 7:74; 10:14; 10:73; 35:39)). One line in that note is worth keeping in view for the whole story: some commentators read the word from a root meaning to come after, so that the human being arrives after every other creature, with every level of creation gathered into his one person.

Al-Jalālayn describes the deputy as the one who carries out God's rulings on the earth, and he names him: this was Adam. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report tafsir_initial carries, adds a piece of history that explains what the angels were thinking. A tribe called al-ḥinn had lived on the earth before, and they had caused damage there, so the angels knew from experience what a resident with free choice could do. The same report says Iblīs belonged to that tribe, that his name was al-Ḥārith, and that the angels were created from light while his kind was created from smokeless fire ((55:15 — **“He created the jinn from a flare of fire”**)).

Al-Saʿdī makes a point about the words of the announcement that rewards a slow reading. God could have said He was placing a deputy on the earth. Instead the verse names what that deputy will need and will sometimes misuse: a free hand. A creature of that sort can be trusted with a garden, and he can also break it. That is the risk in the appointment, and the verse does not hide it.

**THE OBJECTION THE ANGELS RAISED**

***“They asked ˹Allah˺, “Will You place in it someone”*** ***“who will spread corruption there”*** Al-Saʿdī explains that the angels spoke according to what they expected, not according to what they knew. He also notices the order of their question. Corruption is a wide word, and bloodshed is named after it, which singles out killing from everything else that damages a community. The verse puts the smaller word first and the graver one second, and the arrangement shows how heavy the killing of a life is in the scale of the Book.

Al-Jalālayn records the background the angels were drawing on: the jinn who had lived on the earth before and spilled blood there, and who had been driven out to the islands and the mountains when God sent the angels against them. The angels were not inventing a worry. They were reading history and asking whether the same story was about to be told again.

Tazkirul Qur'an keeps the same question but moves it into general terms that any reader can follow. A creature with a free will is a creature who can corrupt himself and hurt others, and the record of human history has answered the angels' question in the affirmative many times over. God knew that. He had a reason for the appointment, and the answer He gives at the end of the verse is not that the worry is imaginary. It is that the account has another side the angels cannot see.

Ibn Kathīr adds a note on the honour hidden inside the complaint. The angels were not jealous of mud and they were not objecting to God's decision. They were asking a question about wisdom, and the answer they received is the one every honest questioner receives. The Book records both halves of the exchange so that the reader learns two things at once: it is permitted to ask, and the answer may be a single sentence about knowledge beyond reach.

**WHAT THE ANGELS SAID OF THEMSELVES**

***“and shed blood while we glorify Your praises”*** ***“and proclaim Your holiness?””*** Al-Saʿdī explains both verbs the angels use about themselves. To glorify God with praise is to declare Him free of everything that does not suit Him, with a declaration that carries praise. To proclaim His holiness carries a second possible meaning, and he gives both readings. It can mean that the angels declare God holy, with the little preposition marking that their worship is for Him alone and free of any other motive. Or it can mean that they purify their own selves for Him, cleaning their inner lives with good qualities such as love of God, fear of Him and reverence for Him, and clearing out the bad ones.

The asbāb al-nuzūl material and al-Baghawī's commentary record the unspoken half of the angels' case: that their worship never stops and never spoils, so why hand the earth to a creature who will do the opposite? Al-Saʿdī says the angels were describing their own continuous service as a way of honouring God, not as a way of promoting themselves.

Kashānī reads the scene with the imagery of the Sufi tradition.
