He describes a world at rest before any dust settled on it, where every created thing was steady in its obedience and each one was content with its place. Then the ocean of mercy began to move, and a creature was planned who could love God by choice rather than by nature. On his reading the angels were measuring the world they knew, and God was about to make something the universe had no sample of.

Think of a factory where every machine runs at the same speed and the manager announces that a new worker will be hired to run one bench by hand. The staff point out that machines do not make mistakes. The manager knows that the point of the new bench is not speed. It is a pair of hands that can choose.

**THE ANSWER THAT CLOSES THE SUBJECT**

***“Allah responded, “I know what you do not know.””*** Al-Saʿdī explains the ground of the answer. The angels had spoken from what they expected, and God knows the outward and the hidden. He also knows the good that will come out of creating this deputy: the prophets, the truthful, the witnesses who die for what they know, and the righteous who carry the earth for a season. Not one of them would have existed if the appointment had not been made.

Al-Jalālayn lists the same goods in his own words: the benefits of placing Adam on the earth, and the fact that his descendants would include both the obedient and the disobedient, so that justice would have something to work with. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the sentence short and lets it carry: God replied that He knew the deep wisdom behind the creation of these creatures and behind their appointment as guardians, and the angels did not.

The answer has one property that makes it worth carrying into every argument a person has with a difficult decree. It is not a refusal to explain. It is a statement that the explanation is larger than the questioner's current position, and that the One who gave the order is already in possession of the end of the story. A reader who takes that seriously will find that it changes the way he asks his own questions about the world.

---
## Verse 2:31

> He taught Adam the names of all things, then He presented them to the angels and said, “Tell Me the names of these, if what you say is true?”

**THE LESSON ADAM WAS GIVEN**

The verse names the lesson given to the first human being: ***“He taught Adam the names of all things”***, Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the lesson in the plainest terms: God taught Adam the names of everything, living and otherwise, along with their pronunciation and their meanings. Al-Jalālayn says the same and adds that the knowledge was placed into his heart. The verse does not describe a class with a teacher standing at a board. It describes a person receiving the basic equipment of a mind that can speak about the world.

Ibn ʿAbbās, in his commentary, reads the names as the names of the creatures and the descendants who would come after Adam, and he gives an example that makes the list concrete: a cooking pot, a plate, a saucer. tafsir_initial gathers the different positions the early scholars took, since the verse left the list open. Ibn ʿAbbās read the names as the words people use when they talk to one another, such as man, sea, mountain. Mujāhid read them as the names of everything. Others read them as the names of the angels. Al-Ṭabarī preferred the reading that they are the names of Adam's descendants, and he gave a grammatical reason: the pronoun used for the things presented is the masculine plural, which Arabic uses for beings with reason.

Al-Qurṭubī opens his commentary on this verse with a grammar point and then moves to something larger. The verb means to make known, and the teaching here may have come by direct inspiration or through the angel Gabriel. He records the observation of the Sufi teachers, who noted that Adam knew the names by God's instruction and kept them by God's protection, but that he later forgot what he had been told, in the verse about the covenant he broke ((20:115 — **“And indeed, We had taken a covenant from Adam before, but he forgot”**)). Knowledge given and knowledge kept are two different gifts, and this verse is about the first.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws a conclusion about language itself from this episode. Since the first human being was taught the names rather than inventing them, the position of Imām al-Ashʿarī is supported: language is a creation of God rather than a human convention that appeared by accident. Human beings then developed it into thousands of forms over the centuries, and every one of those forms rests on a capacity that was placed in the first man by his Maker.

Think of a child who walks into a workshop and can already name every tool, its use and its danger, without being told twice. The workman who owns the shop would notice. That is roughly the position Adam holds in this scene, and the angels are the ones who notice it.

**THE DISPLAY BEFORE THE ANGELS**

***“then He presented them to the angels”*** Al-Jalālayn reads the presentation as a display of the named things in front of the angels, and he notes that the names concerned intellectual beings for the most part. Al-Mukhtaṣar describes the same act as putting the things before the angels and telling them to give the names.

Ibn Kathīr places the scene in the order of the story as the Qur'an tells it. The discussion in these verses comes before the command to prostrate, and he explains why: the point of the arrangement is to show the rank of Adam and the limits of what the angels knew when they asked their question. The presentation was not a quiz for its own sake. It was the answer to the objection in the previous verse, delivered as evidence rather than as an argument.

Al-Saʿdī keeps the sequence of cause and effect in view. The angels had compared themselves with the creature being appointed, on the basis of what each party did. God answered the comparison with a different measure, and the measure was knowledge. Everything else in this passage follows from that choice: the teacher's seat, the pupils' answer, and the prostration that comes next.

**THE COMMAND THE ANGELS COULD NOT MEET**

***“and said, “Tell Me the names of these”*** ***“if what you say is true?””*** Al-Jalālayn explains the conditional clause and what it refers back to. The angels had said, in effect, that God would not create anything more knowledgeable than they were, or that they were more deserving of the appointment. So the instruction was a reproach: name these things if the claim holds. He notes that the sentence leaves the answer unspoken, because the verse that follows supplies it immediately.

Al-Baghawī records the reasoning behind that claim in the words the commentators transmitted. When the angels heard the announcement, they said among themselves that their Lord would not create a creature more honoured to Him than they were, and that if He did, they were still the more knowledgeable, since they were created first and had seen what the new creature had not. That is a perfectly reasonable argument, and the verse dismantles it with a table of objects standing in front of them.

Al-Mukhtaṣar states the purpose plainly: the display was made to establish the standing of Adam, and the question was asked to show that the angels were not in a position to make the comparison they had made. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws attention to one small difference in wording that carries the meaning. The angels were told to tell God; Adam is later told to tell them. In the first sentence the angels are being questioned. In the second, a human being is given the seat of a teacher and the angels are placed in the seats of students.

A junior clerk who has told the board that the new accountant knows nothing is asked to take the man's ledger and read a page of it aloud. The question in the verse is that ledger. Its whole point is that it is in front of everybody and the reading is short.

---
## Verse 2:32

> They replied, “Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**THE WORDS THE ANGELS ANSWERED WITH**

The angels answer with a confession: ***“They replied, “Glory be to You!”*** Al-Jalālayn reads the first word as an act of declaring God far above what does not suit Him, and he adds what the angels were clearing away: the idea that anyone could object to God's decision. Al-Mukhtaṣar adds that the sentence carries an admission of their own limits and a recognition that God's judgement and His law cannot be doubted.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the word as an expression of return, spoken in the sense of turning back from what they had said, and the commentators record that reading alongside the first. Al-Qurṭubī draws the moment out. The angels answered the command to name the things by stating that nobody besides God knows the unseen, and they did not reach for knowledge they did not have. That is the behaviour of a created being who knows where his information comes from.

The verse records the angels answering a question with a confession, and the confession is the correct answer to this particular test. They were not asked what they had read or heard. They were asked to produce knowledge that lives in the hand of God alone, and the honest reply to that demand is the one they gave.

**WHAT THEY ADMITTED ABOUT KNOWLEDGE**

***“We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise.””*** Al-Jalālayn explains the closing words as a statement about God's knowledge and His wisdom together. Nothing escapes His knowledge. His wisdom means that everything He does has a reason, even when the reason has not been shown to the one asking the question. Al-Mukhtaṣar words it as the angels' acceptance: God is the Knowing from whom nothing is hidden, and the Wise in His decrees and His laws.

Al-Qurṭubī builds a rule out of the angels' reply, and it is one of the most useful sentences in the passage. He says that anyone who is asked about a matter he does not know should say: God knows best, and I do not know. The angels did it, the prophets did it, and the leading scholars of the community did it. He then notes the sharp contrast with the ignorant man among us, who fabricates an answer to protect his reputation. The difference between the two positions is not intelligence. It is honesty.

Ibn Kathīr places the same reply inside the larger lesson of the passage, which is the excellence God gave Adam above the angels in knowledge. The angels are not diminished by the scene. They keep their rank and their worship, and they gain something as well: an increase in knowledge, delivered by a human being, since Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that even the knowledge of angels can grow.

Al-Qurṭubī adds a grammatical note that widens the sentence. The phrase about what God has taught can be read as except the things You taught us, or as except Your teaching of us, so that the angels name the act itself as the only thing they possess. Either reading keeps them in the same position, and it is the position every creature holds before the One who knows all things: a borrower, not an owner.

Think of a technician called to fix a machine he has never seen. He could open the casing and guess. Instead he says the engineer who built it knows the design, and he will wait for the manual. A confident man can say that sentence, and the verse records a group of beings whose ranks do not tempt them to pretend otherwise.

## Verse 2:177

> Righteousness is not in turning your faces towards the east or the west. Rather, the righteous are those who believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Books, and the prophets; who give charity out of their cherished wealth to relatives, orphans, the poor, ˹needy˺ travellers, beggars, and for freeing captives; who establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and keep the pledges they make; and who are patient in times of suffering, adversity, and in ˹the heat of˺ battle. It is they who are true ˹in faith˺, and it is they who are mindful ˹of Allah˺

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:213

> Humanity had once been one community ˹of believers before they lost faith˺. Then Allah raised prophets as deliverers of good news and as warners, and revealed to them the Scriptures in truth to judge among people regarding their disputes. And no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who received them after clear proofs had come to them—out of jealousy. Then Allah, by His grace, has guided the believers to the truth regarding those disputes. And Allah guides whoever He wills to the Straight Path

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:229

> Divorce may be retracted twice, then the husband must retain ˹his wife˺ with honour or separate ˹from her˺ with grace. It is not lawful for husbands to take back anything of the dowry given to their wives, unless the couple fears not being able to keep within the limits of Allah. So if you fear they will not be able to keep within the limits of Allah, there is no blame if the wife compensates the husband to obtain divorce. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. And whoever transgresses the limits of Allah, they are the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:249

> When Saul marched forth with his army, he cautioned: “Allah will test you with a river. So whoever drinks ˹his fill˺ from it is not with me, and whoever does not taste it—except a sip from the hollow of his hands—is definitely with me.” They all drank ˹their fill˺ except for a few! When he and the ˹remaining˺ faithful with him crossed the river, they said, “Now we are no match for Goliath and his warriors.” But those ˹believers˺ who were certain they would meet Allah reasoned, “How many times has a small force vanquished a mighty army by the Will of Allah! ## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:283

> If you are on a journey and a scribe cannot be found, then a security can be taken. If you trust one another, then ˹there is no need for a security, but˺ the debtor should honour this trust ˹by repaying the debt˺—and let them fear Allah, their Lord. And do not conceal the testimony, for whoever conceals it, their hearts are indeed sinful. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you do

## Verse 2:285

> The Messenger ˹firmly˺ believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. They ˹all˺ believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. ˹They proclaim,˺ “We make no distinction between any of His messengers.” And they say, “We hear and obey. ˹We seek˺ Your forgiveness, our Lord! And to You ˹alone˺ is the final return.”

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
